Radium wets tipon the chemical constitu• ents of glass, porcelain, and paper, impart- ing to them a violet tinge, changes white phosphorus to yellow, oxygen to ozone, affects photograph plates, and produces many other curious chemical changes.

EMBROIDERY.

Embroidery used in fashioning the new high collars and cuffs for morning costumes is of the finest in needlework and fabric. Both the open embroidery, that which shews an elaborate cut- out pattern, and the fine close work which orna- mented frocks of a century ago, are liked. There is so great a demant . for the genuine old em- broideries, declares a writer in the Mark Lane Express, that women who count these among their choicest heirlooms, are having bits of the embroidery cut out and mounted on new linen and shaped into collar and cuff sets. The antique embroidery invariably lasts longer than the transparent linen upon which it has been worked, and it lends itself very readily to this transfer.. Those who do not own old em- broideries have to content themselves with the new, which reproduce as nearly as possible the designs and style of the embroidery worn by our great-grandmothers. Two FASHIONABLE SKIRTS.

Pictured in our illustration are two very smart and up-to-date skirts, one for regular walking wear, the other of a more dressy indoor type. The first is realised in navy serge of a rather hard and wiry ,weave, and is cut with five gores, each well sloped out to give ample width at the bottom. At the back the fulness is arranged in an inverted pleat, which is firmly machined down for some little distance below the waist. A lino of machining outlines the hem, and gives a suit- able finish to the skirt. The second sketch shews

The great old Tudor mansion looked solemn in the moonlight. Below, the terraces and the big flower-garden seemed, as it were, sleeping ia the shadow—then came a moonlit expanse of park, whose dark patches here and there were clumps of elms and beeches, with their black shadows lying on the grass. It was such a atill spring night that Eve, the slim girl waiting in the shrubbery, listening, motionless, heard the t quick beating of her anxious heart as well as every faint rustle of some waking creature—rabbit, rat, mouse, or bird—in the brushwood. She had been early at her tryst with the man who had so suddenly, but so entirely, become the lover she worshipped as a god, for whom she felt ready, if need arose, even to die. Moments of tension became minutes, minutes quarters of an hour; she heard them chime with a dread akin to agony—for the Honourable Captain Reginald Burleigh had snatched a stolen minute with her that day in the upper corridor, where the maids' workroom was, to imperiously demand that meeting. "You must come, Evie, I have a great favour to ask you. You said you would do anything in the world for me, and I am going to put you to the test," he had said, with a fond look and a hasty kiss, before he hurried off beyond the baize door within whose boundary he had no business to be. Since then she had been on tenter-hooks. What could it be he meant to ask? She had wondered, and was wondering now, as her eyes fixed on the glowing windows of the blue drawing-room— a room which opened upon the terrace and which Lady Burleigh, Reginald's aunt, generally elected, to occupy evenings, when the house- party was moderately small—she longed and listened and watched, her hands nervously clasped to her breast. He had been so passionate, so additionally tender of late—could he, did he mean to ask her to elope with him? She started—there was a crashing sound in the shrubbery—she gave an involuntary cry as he sprang upon the path, which startled a night bird into breaking covert and soaring upward and away with a weird, expostulating wail. "What, frightened? You little goose!" He took her in his arms and kissed her gently, with an almost paternal solicitude,which the innocent, trusting girl had awakened in him' case-hardened, b military worldling as he believed himself to be. "Come, let us go to that old bench further on, our bench, where we first told each other— well, you know what!" Gently, with many fond words, he caressingly drew her along to the spot—a little rose-garden in the wood where a former Lady Burleigh had made a cemetery for her pets. Her ladyship's favourite hunter lay below an oblong bed of rose bushes. A row of tiny tombstones under the hedge headed the little grassy mounds, the graves of her pet dog. Captain Burleigh seated himself on the rustic bench also placed there tly his deceased great-aunt, his arm around his "little love," as he had called her. "I didn't half like asking you to meet me her; late, like this, dear, but my news wouldn't wait; and I felt you ought to hear it from me— certainly not from anyone else! Evie, I am going to be married." He believed, when a shock had to be given, it was best to launch a thunderbolt. But he wondered how the girl would take it. To his surprise she remained motionless, an inanimate lump leaning against him. He peered anxiously into her face. At first he could hardly discern her features—a cloud was passing across the moon—but as the pale, pure light shone down once more he saw that she was livid—her starting eyes shone with a mad lustre. "Evie—my darling!" he wildly exclaimed. "Don't look like that—speak—speak ! " His loved voice—A was a rich, mellow-speak- ing voice, and had been pleasant in the ears of other women and girls before it had assisted to enslave poor Evie—roused her. She shivered, and drew away from him. "You—yes, you are to be married—l hear," she said in a dull, even tone. "When ? " "Ask me why, rather," he passionately said, drawing her imperiously nearer. "Evie, I am not a brute, I am not a wretch, nor am I a blackguard! I love you, and you know it, but I cannot, and would not if I could, marry you. I am poor, and in debt, and so is my old dad—a 'regular good old spendthrift he is! lam bound to help him. The girl lam going to marry is an heiress—will be a great heiress by-and-bye. But putting the money question aside, Evie, supposing even I had loads of oof instead of being the abject beggar I am, you would be miserable if I married you—my aunt's third lady's-maid and her housekeeper's niece. Why, we should be hated, scorned, cut, insulted, hounded out of all decent society! It would be sheer madness. I would not expose you to such a thing! We met and loved—we could not help it—and now we must say good-by. !It cuts both ways. It is agony to me, I tell you, but it is my duty. I shall go and marry Gwendolen Field and try to be a good husband to her, and you will do the same by your promised husband, your Cousin Rob—" He stopped short. She sprang up suddenly. "I will never lOok him in the face again," she hoarsely said. "Dare to say his name to me! You said "—she halted to breathe, and pressed her hands tightly upon her chest—"you said—you had a favour—to ask me—what is it?" "To forget I ever existed, dear," he said, earnestly. "Do not give me a thought after to. night; I am not worth it." She stood staring at him; then she gave a shrill laugh, which seemed to chill the blood in his veins. "Forget? Oh, Heavens!" she wailed. Then she rushed away. He was alone. 'I have nothing to reproach myself with," he *old himself as he went slowly back to the house. "I don't know a single fellow who would have been as straight, in my place, with my feeling for the girl, as I have been." IL

RECORDS OF THE EARTHQUAKE CURVES by a continual series of sparks passing be- tween it and the metal support over which a paper is caused to slide, thus perforating the -Latter with a number of small holes. The main advantage claimed for this arrangement is that the pendulum is entirely free in space, being subject to no friction effect, while read- ings can be obtained at any distance from the point at which the seismograph is in- stalled, and at the very moment the earth- quaxe is taking place. The apparatus is readily adapted to the pendulums of most existing types of seismograph, and will give records of any desired sensitiveness.

King, M.P., in a brief address at th annual meeting of ths Knutsford Liberal Associatiol held at the Manchester Reform Club on SaturcllY reviewed some of the legislative and adthinistratity work done by the Government, and showed how both kinds of work it had benefited the masses of tl people. MR. CROSFIELD'S WEDDING.

LIVLIPOOL STAFF ENTERTAINED.

Owing to pressure on our space last week we were only able to briefly announce that on the previous Saturday, Mr. A. H. Crosfield, M.P., entertained to dinner at the Victoria Restaurant, Liverpool, the staff and employees of the Liverpool branch of Messrs. Joseph Crosfield and Sons, Limited. The dinner was given in connection with Mr. Crosfield's recent marriage, and the whole of the employees at the Liverpool Works were present. On joining the party at dinner, Mr. , Crosfield was greeted with cheers. Dinner over, Mr. Dolan conveyed to Mr. Croetleld the thanks of the guests for his kindness, and their sincere wishes that Mrs. Crosfield and he would have great happiness in their future lives. (Applause.) Mr. McGregor, in following Mr. Dolan, remarked that Mr. Crosfield's kindness in inviting them to , dinner was but another instance of the characteristic generosity of the Crosfield family to their employees —and he trusted that Mr. and Mrs. Crosfield would be spared to enjoy many many years of married happiness and prosperity. (Cheers.) As soon as Mr. McGregor remmed his seat, cheers were given for Mr. and Mrs. Crosfield, and "He's a jolly good fellow" was sung with great heartiness. Mr. Crosfield then addressed the party, thanking them for the warmth and geniality of their welcome, and saying that it gave him great pleasure to take the opportunity he had that evening of expressing on behalf of his wife, as well as on his own account, their thanks for the artistic and very charming present which, in the kindness of their hearts, the staff and employees at the Liverpool branch of the firm of Crosfield had sent them—which they would always value and appreciate. (Cheers.) "A near relative of mine, who is a very witty man, tells me," continued Mr. Crosfield," that whenever he is asked to speak at any sort of nuptial ceremony, he invariably addresses his friends as follows, I have only been married once, and I hop 3 I may never be married again." (Much laughter.) Well gentlemen, I have only had a short experience of matrimony, but I too heartily subscribe to those sentiments.' (Renewed laughter.) " I regret," (continued Mr. Crosfield," that I have not a chance of meeting you more frequently in ' the Liverpool works—but I appreciate the more this hearty and pleasant gathering this evening and I often hear from my brother, Captain Crosfield—(cheers)— and others, of the good progress of our manufacture and business at the Liverpool works. (Hear. hear.) I have much pleasure in congratulating you all, and especially Mr. Fox (cheers) and his able successor Mr. Dolan (cneers) on the excellent work and good manage- ment at Liverpool, on the good standards maintained in manufacture, and everything that conduces to good trade, for the benefit of all here." (Cheers.) Mr. Crostield then referred to the very important part! taken in the building up and development of the firm's busimess by the late Mr. George trosfield, and tce the ' emphasis his uncle always laid on the maintenance of , good and reliable standards of quality. He had not " (said Mr. Crosfield) "a dim notion, a vague iclea,a doubt- ' ful impression of the usefulness of such good and reliable standards in building up a business. No—from keen business insight and practical experience he had acquired a firm grip of that fundamental fact— and that, in years gone by had not a little to do with his success in business. Another thing my uncle used to say " (continued Mr. Crosfield) "was the nearer home you can sell your goods, the better. Well, during the early part of my business career, from 1884 to 1895, when I devoted myself very closely to the soap trade, and put in some fib hours a week at it, I laid this maxim of Mr. George Crosfield well to heart, and the greater part of my time and energies during that very critical period in the history of this trade,were devoted to building up a new kind of soap trade at home which was needed to replace the older-fashioned class of trade which was fast declining ; but I also spent a large part of my time then in doing my best to assist the develop- ment of these new branches of our trade in which you are specially interested in Liverpool, and lam right glad that the last five and twenty years have seen this important development in the firm's export trade. (Cheers.) That is satisfactory, gentlemen from a com- mercial point of view. (Hear, hear.) But I think every- one in this room rray also congratulate himself on being connected with a business which is not only scientific and interesting on its technical side, but also deeply interesting on its commercial side. (Hear, hear.) It has often been said that soap is a measure of civilisa- tion, and to a great measure that is true—so that, in sending our goods to different parts of the world, and in many cases to countries with coloured populations in a somewhat backward stage of development, I think we may, in effect, claim to be pioneers of civilisation. (Cheers.) Cleanliness comes next to godliness and we seepmakers—modest people that we are— (laughter)—think we rank next to parsons! (Laughter.) Might we not say then—without blowing our own trumpet too loudly, that in manufacturing for many of the different nationalities to whom our goods are shipped abroad, if on the one hand we are pioneers of civilisation, on the other (to paraphrase a modern expression with which you are familiar) we are also the missionaries of trade." (Hear. hear, and cheers.) After referring to the time when he used to take specimens of the fi'rm's handicraft and manufacture, if not literally from John o' Groats to Lands End, at all events North, South, East and West through the country, Mr. Crosfield emphasised the importance of that which as called " get up " plays in trade. If this is important," he said, " in the home trade, it is, if possible, still More important in the export trade. There, as we all know, in a great many cases we are selling to people who have little knowledge, or even no knowledge at all of English, and therefore,in order that they may recognise a good article and ask for it again it is of the utmost importance that our Trade Marks should appear clearly, neatly and legibly on bars, tablets and boxes of soap alike. (Hear, hear.) I can assure you that every one in our works by hearing in mind that important fact, and acting upon it, can give us direct and effective assistance ia the development of the business. (Cheers.) I hope that as civilisation pronresses, the demand for English soap in foreign markets will continue to increase, so that all firms engaged in the trade on the Mersey may be kept busy, to the benefit of the firms themselves and every one connected with them, and therefore to the benefit also of the old County Palatine of Lancashire." (Cheers.) In conclusion Mr.Crostield expressed his regret that Mrs. Crosfield could not be with them that evening owing to the fact that an attack of influenza had made it impossible fcr her at the time to go out at night. He a gain thenked them on her behalf, and on his own behalf for their very pretty present, and most heartily did he thank them for the genial welcome they had given him that evening. (Loud cheers.) Mr. Fox then rose and expressed his pleaeure at the kindly mention made of Mr. Dolan and himself by Mr. Crostield, and for the way in which that mention bad been received. He was sure that any one, seeing the conditions under which Messrs. Crosfields' employees did their work would realise that they worked for a considerate firm, and, he might add, for the best interests of that firm. (Hear, hear.) Whatever com- fort they might have in their work, be regarded as only carrying out the principles their directors had always supported. (Hear, hear.) He was sure their men would always endeavour to deserve that consideration. He would conclude by thanking them for their kind expressions of good will towards himself and Mr.Dolan. (Cheers.) Mr. Crosfield said he thought they should not con- clude that part of their proceedings without hearing a few words from their friend Mr. Hamilton. Mr. Hamilton, who was warmly greeted, said that as representing the commercial side of the export scan branch of the business, he was sure he might say one and all had the interests of the firm at heart and desired to see it prosper and develop in every direction. Each one could in his own way assist in these develop- ments, and help to hold up the Crosfield flag," and make known the Crosfield open "hand "in every part of the world, nnd if real " true blues " to the firm, they could materially assist in the attainment of a " signal success. (Loud Cheers.) The party then proceeded to the Olympia Music Hall to conclude a pleasant evening, arrangements having been previously made for seats to be reserved for them.

GNATS AND FEVER.

In the Philippine Journal of Science Messrs. Ashburn and Craig give 801110 ac- count of the part played by a species of gnat (culex fatigans) in the spread of the disease known as Dengue fever. They reared the insects from the egg, and found that after they had been allowed to bite a dengue patient they communicated the disease to an individual who vas isolated under a mos- quito net, and not subject to infection _in any other way. No., micro-organisms, however, were found in the infected gnats. and it is therefore concluded that the dengue microbe is ultro-microscopic in size.

THE HAIR AND MENTAL SHOCK.

In a disquisition on the nature of the pig- ment of the human hair, the. Lancet admits that it is not easy to explain exactly why alterations in colour occasionally take place during a serious illness, or why it may be- come whitened even in a few hours of mental disturbance. It mentions a somewhat re- markable instance of such disturbance which has come to its knowledge, where the cpposite was the case. The patient, a lady, suffered from occasional severe attacks of mental depression, and with each attack her hair, which is naturally a dark auburn, turned quite black. When she recovered the hair resum2d its natural colour.

===== 0002601_19080307_art0019.txt | included=true | included =====
THE LIGHTNING FLASH.

Remarkable studies of the nature of light- ning have been made by a gifted Danish- American experimenter, Alexander Larsen. Not only has it been found that lightning flashes are made up of innumerable shorter flashes, and that streaks of lightning are really linked—i.e., chain lightning—but the existence of black or invisible lightning has been ascertained. Larsen took photographs of lightning with a common hand camera, and considered that if a camera were so geared as to be moved during lightning flashes the duration of the flash might be determined and its nature made clearer. He rigged up a revolving table, upon which he placed cameras in such positions as to catch lightning flashes at various phases. It was soon discovered that what is called a flash of lightning is in reality a succession of flashes, deceiving the eye with an appear- ance of oneness.

CAN ANIMALS DISTINGUISH COLOURS? Wallace has asserted that the fact that, certain plants bear fruit of brilliant colours is due to their preservation; the animals attracted by these colours break the fruits from the trees or plants, carry them off, and thus indirectly assist in the dissemination of the seeds which they contain over large tracts of land. And this function of selection on the part of animals presupposes in them a certain sense of colour. Still, scientific documents in support of these suppositions are rare. Dr. Dahl. alluding to the scarcity of them, relates some interesting experi- ments which he made with a monkey. He coloured some sweets with a certain coloured dye, and some bitter substances with that of another colour, and declares that after a few attempts the monkey learnt to leave, without even tasting, those articles of food which were coloured with the dye, and which indicated bitter - tasting substances, and seized at once upon those which indicated sweets. Varying the experiments sufficiently, he found that the monkey distinguished all the different colours readily, save only dark blue. Dr. Dahl calls attention to the fact that Mayer has stated that many savage tribes cannot, distinguish dark blue from black, and that even children distinguish this colour later than all others.

===== 0002601_19080307_art0020.txt | included=true | included =====
SUN'S VARIABLE HEAT OUTPUT.

Mr. Watts, the American meteorologist, in a recent lecture said it must be clear to any- one who gave the matter thought that if the sun's radiant output were a constant, with the land and water masses of the earth being what they are now from all historic periods, the variation in climatic conditions from year to year should be insignificant. As a matter of fact, however, we know that the variations are often of an extraordinary character. In any one given year, for in- stance, the central regions of the United States might experience a Ammer of extra- ordinary dryness and intense heat, while the next year the very same region might ex- perience a cool and wet summer, or a cool and dry summer, or any combination pos- sible in weather conditions. The relation of such contrasts in -seasons to the solar varia- tions, he declared, was being worked out by a number of specialists—among them being Professor F. H. Bigelow, of the United States Weather Bureau and he said in general the belief which was obtaining cre- dence among meteorologists was that not only must the variation in the quantity in the solar radiation be taken into account, but a variation in the quality, in the matter of its electrical and magnetic as well as mechanical effects.

ELECTRICAL RECORDER FOR

EARTHQUAKES.

An apparatus in which 'electricity is em- ployed to record earthquake curves has been designed by Professor R. Goldschmidt, of the University of Brussels, says the Times. A pendulum carries one or two pairs of diame- trically-opposed coils of this copper wire, in front of which there are arranged other coils in perfect alignment with the former, wound with a smaller number of turns of thicker copper wire. The coils of thick wire ere at- tached to a frame resting on the ground so as to partake in any earthquake motion, and as the coils fixed to the central metal mass, owing to the inertia of the latter, tend to re- main at rest, the relative distances between the two sets of coils will be alternately in- creased and reduced, thus setting up in the coils fixed to the pendulum alternate currents representative of the

DiTENSITY OF EARTHQUAKE OSCILLATIONS.

The thick wire coils are traversed by low in- tensity alternate currents produced by a small rotary transformer from two sets of five accumulator cells, As the coils carried by the seismograph pendulum are arranged in series with a galvanometer of high sensi- tiveness, the readings of the latter 'at any moment indicate the actual intensity of earthquake motion, deflections being nil in the state of rest, and, as each group of coils should be provided with its own galvano- meter, a horizontal-perpendicular seismo- graph should be fitted with two galvanometers. BOROUGH AUDITORS.

Result of the Election.

Very little interest wns taken in the annual election of two auditors for the borough which took place on Monday at the Gymnasium. The candidates were Messrs. Laurence Bolton and Ebenezer England (present holders of the offices), and Mr. W. Chrimes, commission agent, Knutsford-road. Many people were even unaware that a poll was taking place, for the candidates are not allowed to spend a penny on stating' their claims to the suffrages of the electorate. When the result of the poll was declared, only about 20 people had assembled. The counting commenced shortly after eight o'clock in the Council Chamber at the Town Hall, and was concluded in about 15 or 20 minutes. The Mayor (who was accompanied by the Town Clerk and Deputy Town Clerk) acted as returning officer. The result was declared inside the room as follows : Mr. Bolton Mr. England Mr. Chrimes 122

There were 13 spoiled papers. Mr. BOLTON in proposing a vote of thanks to tho Mayor for his services, thanked all the officials for the able manner in which the election had been conducted, and said be was particularly obliged to those 309 persons who had voted for him without canvassing. Mr. ENGLAND seconded the motion, and said the Mayor's presence graced every meeting over which be presided, and acted upon them as a ray of sunshine. The election, like its predecessors, had been very well con- ducted.

Mr. CHRIMES supported, and said be approved of everything that had been said by the two previous speakers. He bad met with nothing hut courtesy, and was particularly obliged to all who had voted for him. He had no doubt he would have better luck next time.

The motion having been carried, The MANOR briefly returned thanks and formally announced the result of the poll to the people outside the building. No speeches were made.

THE WAR AGAINST CONSUMPTION.

A unique effort for helping to stay the scourge of consumption has been devised by the Gentlewoman. This enterprising journal has just received exclusive authority to publish in Great Britain and the Colonies a series of Royal artist postcards reproduced from original drawings ,by the German Emperor, tho late Queen Carole of Saxony, the late King and the Queen of Portu- gal, H.R.H. Mathilde Princess of Saxony, Prince Eugene of Sweden, Countess of Flanders, Archduchess Joseph of Austria, Princess Leo- pold of Hohenzollern, Princess Waldemar of Denmark, Princess Feodora of Schleswig-Hol- stein, and the Princess of Vendome. The idea seems to hare originated with Queen Carole of Saxony, and the effort is international in its scope. An eighth part of the proceeds will be set aside as a donation to some English charity, a further proportion being paid to a charity for the cure of consumption in the native country of each Royal artist.

HATS AND THEIR WEARERS.

Some girls can wear the maddest hats and look both sane and charming in them, declares a Globe writer. One of our young actresses, for instance, is in the habit of wearing a helmet composed of ermine planted anyhow on her lovely fair hair. Again a noted beauty of our aristocracy wears a turban composed of the breasts of some Brazilian bird, the feathers green and blue and purple, the tints shoaling into each other in a marvellous fashion. From the front of this toque issues a treble plume, consisting of pen feathers, the shortest of which measures over a quarter of a yard. On some women this would be eccentric indeed, but on the beautiful fair-haired woman who adopts the style the effect is perfect. But alas for the poor Brazilian bird! A SMART SHIRT BLOUSE.

Then he locked himself in alone for another briefer period of silence, and then—a report rang through the silent house and shook the summer sky. The valet, leaping up the stairs, was the first to enter, but what the valet saw all the others saw a few minutes later. Lord Herrisdale was stretched on the floor with a bullet-hole in his left temple. On a table, signed and witnessed, but also enclosed and scaled, lay what proved to be a freshly made will and testament, or, rather, a codicil, bequeathing fifty thousand pounds of the dead man's private property te Catherine Presteign. _ _ _

THE BOROUGH AUDITORS.

The MAYOR announced that Messrs. Laurence Bolton and Ebenezer England had been elected the elective auditors for the borough. He had appointed Councillor Wilson as Mayor's auditor.

THE MEDICAL INSPECTION OF SCHOOL CHILDREN. THIS PROPOSED NEW APPOINTNIENTS.

Some discussion took place on the Education Cora- mittee's minutes with reference to the medical inspec- tion of school children under the Education (Adminis- trative Provisions) Act, 1907. Councillor EvANs,chairman of the Health Committee, moved an amendment that the minutes relating to that question be not confirmed. He pointed out that the Board of Education bad issued a memorandum and recommended that the education authority (not the Education Committee) "should use to the utmost extebt the existing machinery of medi d and sanitary I administration," and" that the work should be tried I out in intimate conjunction with the public health authorities and under the direct supervision of the Medical Officer of Health." Since the passing of the Education Act the Council had given considerable attention to the work named in the memorandum, and for which the Education Committee wera seeking to I make provision. Since the appointment of the present medical officer and since October last, all the elemen- tary schools of the borough had been visited, and 688 children had been medically examined.' More than that, with their present staff of officials, there had been paid during 1037, 2,588 visits to the homes of scholars who had been reported by the Educa- tion Committee as being away from school in conse- quence of illness, and those figures 'id not include the number of visits paid on account of notifi- able and infectious disease. The medical examination of children was not a question of education, and was a matter outside the province of the Education Corn-1 mittee. The Health Committee believed that the medical inspection of children would be more efficiently and economically conducted by the organized staff under its control than by setting up an outside or inde- pendent department, or by the appointment of special or independent officials. The Medical Officer of Health did not approve of the proposals of the Education Committee. It was true that he reported on the suggestions made to him by the sub-committee of the Education Committee. ~He reported as to the best way in which their recommends- tions could be carried out. He did not report in favour of them. On the contrary he had previously reported to the Health Committee that, in his opinion, the medical inspection of children should be placed in the hands of the health department under the Health Com- mittee. Since the introduction of the (Education Act, and prior to it, the staff under the care of the Health Committee had been considerably increased and perhaps never had the inspection of the town generally received better attention than that at the present time. (Hear, hear.) They believed that, under the supervision of the Medical Officer' as the requirements of the Education Committee became known, there could be added to the staff of inspectors all that would be necessary, either in the appointment of an assistant medical officer of health or in some other economical way, to efficiently carry out the work of the borough. (Hear, bear.) The education rate was becoming no trifling matter. (Hear, hear.) lie was beginning to feel that they could not afford from time to time to pass the Education Committee's Minutes, in which sums of money were involved, without some notice being taken of them by that Council ; and he I said most emphatically that, looking at the economic side, the Health Committee was the channel through which the work should be done. Neither the Health Committee nor the health department was antagonistic to the Education Committee. Their desire was to work in harmony together so that the objects of the Act might be efficiently carried out to the advantage of the children whom the Act had intended to be benefited by its good and useful pro- visions. (Hear, hear.) Councillor LORD seconded, and said the initial expenditure would be from £3OO to £4OO, and the ' matter could be worked in conjunction with the Health Committee. Councillor STOTT bad no fault to find with the work of the Education Committee, and no one admired it more than he did. He was not going to complain that they had spent too much—in fact, he did not think they had spent sufficient on the lines he could indicate to the Council. He supported the amendment because he felt that the work named was really the work of th) health authority. The new Act said that all children attending elementary schools should be examined, not might be, at least at three periods of their school life, and that meant that during the present year no fewer than 4,000 examinations would have to take place, and next year the number wo2ld be doubled. The work could not be done withonen assistant officer and one or two officials in addition. The work must be taken in lband even if they had to bear the whole of the expense by local taxation. He believed that the whole expense of education and medical inspection should be borne by national taxation, because the areas least able to bear the expense had the heaviest burdens. The Govern- ment— Councillors SINGLETON and ROBERTS objected \to remarks being made about the Government. Councillor STOTT : r am not talking of the Licensing Bill. The MAYOR : Let me conduct the meeting. When Councillor Stott is out of order I will stop him. I have the Town Clerk to advise me. Councillor ROGERS : He is talking about the Licensing Bill. (Laughter.) It should be the ' Educstion not the Licensing Bill. (More laughter.) . .

„ Councillor ROBERTS : ItlS an Councillor STOrr I was not speaking about the Licensing Bill. The MAYOR Keep to the subject and make the speech as short as possible. Councillor &row said he bad been trying to create a public opinion on the subject to influence the Govern- ment to pass legislation on the lines of the Bill they wore now considering, and the matter was, therefore, of great importance to him. He was more concerned about the .cork being efficiently done than as to who should carry it out. (Hear, hear.) If any attempt was made to shelve the work he should do his best at the mc 2tings of the Health Committee and Council to push it forward.

Councillor PEMBERTON said it was evident that the Ilealth Committee were the authority to look after the medical inspection of children, and the Medical OfScer was the best man to supervise the staff engaged. He had a grasp of the homes and the Public Health Act was behind him to remove what required to be removed. In a borough like Warrington, with their education rate, they ought to have more relief from the Govern- ment.

Alderman TINNION said .£5OO was provided in the Education Committee's estimates for the cost of medical inspection. He suggested that the sum should be reduced to £3OO.

Councillor EVANS said he should also suggest that reduction. Councillor FULTON-SMITH said there had been a great deal of tilting at wind mills. In the first place it was assumed that the Education Committee was at variance with the Health Committee. (Cries of "No, no.") There was not a greater mistake ; and he (the speaker) could put the question in a small space. A great deal hung on two words used by Councillor Evans who said distinctly that the Health Com- mittee could carry 'On the projected work both efficiently and economically. Well, then, he (Councillor Fulton-Smith) was sure that every

member of the Education Committee would support him in this—that if the Health Committee were pre- pared to bear the brunt of all the expense of medical inspection (" Cries of No, no" and laughter)—the Education Committee would have no objection. He defied any ordinary man in business to read the regula- tions of the Board of Education and put any other meaning on them than that the work was expected to be done by the education authorities, and that the Board should receive all the reports from them. That was as clear as daylight. The Health Committee had no dealings with the Board of Education as a com- mittee. Why was it that the Medical °Moor did not tell the sub-committee that the work could be much more cheaply done by the Health Committee ? They had the doctor's signature to the report to say that the was bis opinion and his advice to the Education Com- mittee how to carry out the regulation of the Board of Education.

Councillor Evan rose to a point of order. He could not allow that statement to pass. He clearly and dis- tinctly pointed out that the report Dr. Hibbert signed was the report of the special committee and not the report of the education authority. Dr. Hibbert signed it as the best suggestion on the lines named, but not on the lines they were debating that night. Councillor PtILToN-Bitrrx asked that if that were so why did not the doctor when he gave in the report, put a qualifying clause to say that he had already reported to the Health Committee that he did not approve of that? That was the difficulty they were in. He did not think it was quite fair to the sub-committee to be treated in that way. They were business men, and expected to be treated in a business way, but he would certainly recommend that the Health Committee meet the School Management Committee as a body and dis- cuss the question, and, at the same time, ask the Board of Education to send down one of their head officials who had charge of that particular department, and apply for the best means of carrying out their injunc- tion not only economically but efficiently. (Hear, hear.) Councillor EVANS : I submit that there is nothing in my amendment to prevent that being done. After a little further discussion the minutes of the Education Committee were approved with theexception of those relating to the proposed Assistant Medical Officer and the Education Administrative Provisions Act, 1907, which were withdrawn for further considera- tion. _

The minute of the Health Committee relating to the question was as follows :—" The Chairman laid before the committee a proposal of the Education Committee to appoint an assistant medical officer, and health visitor, for the purr mes of the Education (Administra- tive Provisions) Act, 1907, and the Town Clerk advised thereon, when it was resolved that the Town Clerk be requested to write to the Education Commiitee intimat- ing that this committee strongly objected to any such appointments being made by the Education Committee." This was approved without comment. THE SURVEYOR'S DEPARTAENT.

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES.

Golbarne-street Baptist Church.

New Pastor's Settlement.

Sunday MIS a day of particular interest to the workers associated with Golborne-street Baptist Church, as the Rev. R. E. Letheren, the recently appointed pastor, who comes to Warrington from Kingthorpe, entered upon his new duties. He occupied the pulpit both morning and evening and his discourses createda power- ful impression, for he has a very clear and incisive style of delivery and knows how to lay down home trusts in a manner calculated to appeal to all his hearers. At the morning service his subject was " The pastor's purpose" and he took for his text the words: Colossians i. 29, 29. " Whom we preach, warning every man, and teaching every mania all wisdom, that we may present every man perfect in Christ Jesus. Whereunto I labour, striving according to His working, which worketh in me mightily." To preach Christ, he said, was to bring all life—social, commercial, political—in all its parts into a living relationship with life's Lord and King. In the evening Mr. Letheren faced a congregation that fairly filled the chapel. His subject was "The Pastor's Plea" and the text, 2 Car. 1 ii. " Ye also help- ing together by prayer for us." 'He said he proposed to speak of the relations that subsist between a con- gregation and its minister and that both text and sermon were a plea for his people's prayers that to both might be granted grace and wisdom to fulfil them. lit was intensely interesting to notice how in tho open- ing paragraphs of his c zrlier epistle the Apostle seized on figure after figure to try to express his conception of his work amongst the Corinthians. He was a gardener, an architect, one of God's fellow-workers,a minister of Christ, a steward, a father. His work called for an intelligent conception of God's plan, fellowship with God in the fulfilment of it: it could be realized only in dependence on Christ ; it was a great trust: a most intimate relationship with men. Mr. Letherea then passed on to speak of some of a preacher's purposes and plans, his ambitions and aims. The chief business of a minister is to preach. He must do many other things but this first. He desired in his preaching to prove a winner of souls; and as a pastor to bring his ministry into line with the life of his people yet always so as to lead it along the high levels of the life with God. They might reasonably ask that preaching to-day should be instructive, he would like to say educational. That its ideal was not" intellectual repose " but the service of God with the understanding as well as with the spirit. That preaching is barren and worthless which has no other object than to excite a transient emotion or gratify a vagrant fancy. It was his impression that there was a deeper interest in the Bible to-day than in any days since the Reformation. He agreed with Dr. Forsyth who in last year's Yale Lectures on Positive Preaching and the Modern Mind" suggested that there was a demand for the exposition of the Bible, inter- preted by the life of its own times and applied in its changeless force and freshness to the life of to-day. He would endeavour so to preach Christ that his people would abide in Him by faith and love and loyal obedi- ence, and to develop in himself and them the excel- lences of the Christian character. His preaching would be ethical for"though there may be morality where there is not religion, there cannot be religion where there is not morality." He would cease to enforce public duties since human life in its breadth and length belongs to Christ, and citizenship must be christianized. Above all his prettobing would be evangelical, and his evangel the present love of a living God. Having thus dwelt upon what might be expected from the minister, he went on to ask what he might reasonably hope -for from the congregation. The test suggested that first of all they should pray for him. He did not always live upon the sunlit hills, he had his valley of humiliation, his hours of darkness. They would help him by a spiritual attitude as to worship. That it was sometimes said that Nonconformists went to their church to hear sermons and not to worship God. Here are no painted windows, no sensuous music, no historic liturgy, the externals sitnnle even to plain- ness that their approach to God might be the more direct. That Free Churchmen might be the worst of sacerdotalists if they thought of their minister as a priest who did the worship for them. A preacher's position called for their sympathy. When the sermons seemed to have no points or its points were blunted, when his speech was heavy and his thought light he was far more con- scious of it than they were. Sometimes they might not agree with him but he besought them, so Cromwell wrote to the Scots, to think it possible they might be mistaken. lie appealed to their trust and confidence and that they would appreciate all they really could ; to tell him their difficulties ; if he couldn't solve them he could sympathize with them. Be brotherly, he concluded. Let there be no cliques in the Church. Let minister and people be men and brothers. Let our brotherliness break the bounds of our Church and attract men to our fellowship. Let this Church he loysl to Christ and a lover of men for whom Christ died. So shall this relationship upon which we enter to-day, concluded the preacher; be a blessing to us both and to this town. May God's guidance of us be made manifest br His blessing resting upon us from this day forth.

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

RUGBY FOOTBALL

By " Ovalist"

Hands off ! Warrington can still say " hands off to all corners for the N.U. Cup. They hold it at present and they do not intend to part with it except to a superior force. Halifax had an idea they could settle Warrington's pretensions on Saturday, and if the match had been at Thrum Hall they might have done so; but on their own patch of mud Warrington are invincible, and the blue and white forces were routed by 11 points to 7. There were some very anxious moments before the issue was settled, and the losers quite upheld their reputation of being one of the best cup-fighting teams in the Union. Formerly, Batley were the acknowledged champions of cup-tie warfare, hut in the last five years the honour has rested between Halifax and Warrington. Now the former are out of the hunt it looks as if Batley and Warrington may once again have a big influence on the competition. Both are well in ihe running and are playing at the top of thir form.

The game at Warrington on Saturday underwent some Raleidoscopic changes, and many a faint heart palpitated with undue excitement. Halifax twice held the lead, but fortunately when time expired the margin of points rested with Warrington Had there been another quarter of an hour, there is no telling but what the Tykes might have been ahead once more. They had the pleasure of scoring first through a penalty goal by the expert Little, but this moderate success was their undoing. Warrington responded with irresistible dash and some brilliant work in the Halifax quarter saw Fish and Dickenson each put on tries within five minutes. Thomas ought to have landed a goal from the second but failed badly. Still a lead of six points to two at half-time was very satisfactory from the point of view of the home supporters. The second half was opened in a fashion which 'suggested that Warrington were going to sweep the board. Halifax were quite outplayed. but some remarkable defensive work by Little, aided by a s'ice of luck, kept the line intact until nearly half the time had elapsed. Warrington were looked upon as certain winners when the unexpected happened. The usually safe Taylor dallied with a punt at half-way, and the ball was charged down by Joe Riley—the most pertinacious and dangerous player in the Halifax team. He had a long way to go. but the five yards start which he got sufficed, and Riley finished a magni- ficent dribble over the goal-line. One point divided the teams when Little took the goal-kick, and a groan of anguish went up from the dismayed Warringtonians when the ball was seen to dodge the near post and swerve inside. A. point still divided the temns, but the boot was on the other foot.

-]-. -

Halifax now played like giants refreshed, whereas Warrington seemed to have collapsed like a punctured tyre. They blundered and made mistakes which nearly brought further reverses for the next quarter of an hour. It was odds on Halifax winning the match after all when Fortune smiled again on War- rington. Dai Thomas missed a catch, Watkin dribbled through and the speedy Jenkins snapped the ball up and scored behind the posts. It was his first try for Warrington, but it was worth every penny of the Newport man's signing-on fee. The crowd wept tears of joy. Jenkins nearly had a paroxysm in mid•field, and G. Thomas calmly placed a goal which made the issue secure. True it is that the home line had two now escapes in the closing struggle, but all's well that ends well!

There was little to choose between the teams. Halifax scrummaged rather better than Warrington, but were beaten in the loose where Heath and Belton did some fine dribbling. Hilton was the best half on the field, with Brooks a.,good second. Lloyd did all that was expected of him, and showed considerable resource at times. If he could only get the ball out quicker he would he a class half-back. He has certainly imprgved very much in the last few weeks. Halifax had to play second fiddle to the home three- quarters who combined excellently and really won the match. A most brilliant movement which took the ball over the line in the first ten minutes was nullified through Fish having stepped in touch —a very near thing. Watkin and Dai Thomas were both a little weak on defence, but the Warrington man has the credit of Making the winning try possible by a clever dribble. The two full backs both d4stinguished them- selves on a heavy ground, but Little hardly kicked with his old-time power.

The draw for the second round was made on Tues- day and is as follows :

Barrow v. Bramley ; referee, Mr. H. Edmondson. Broughton Rangers v. Wigan ; referee, Mr. R. Robinson, Bradford.

, Batley v. Wakefield Trinity ; referee, Mr. E. Tonge, Swinton.

Runcorn v. Warrington ; referee, lir. W. McCutcheon.

- - Leigh v. York ; referee, Mr. H. Farrar, Keighley. Aferthyr Tydvil v. Whitehavea Reei ; referee, Mr. F. Turner, Warrington.

Hunslet v. Oldham; referee, Mr. F. Kennedy, Broughton Rangers.

Hti-11 v. Salford ; referee. Mr. J. H. Smith, Widnes.

Having already beaten Runcorn twice this season, and unce at Runcorn in a Lancashire Cup-tie, War- rington will have confidencs in their ability to succeed on Saturday next. They cannot grumble at the draw, seeing that Oldham have to co to Hunslet, Wigan to Broughton, and Salford to Hull. All the strongest clubs are thus penalised in a manner which their weaker brethren will consider poetic judgment. I expect to find Warrington in the third round. They have one advantage over most visiting teams, they will have as many supporters at Runcorn as the home club!

THE LEAGUE,

Warrington returned to League football on Tuesday when Bradford were visitors. Warrington i played the full cup team and gave a display which was remarkable for accurate combination and speedy I running. Taylor and Dickenson never played better They completely scattered the opposition and helped to run up a score of 4 goals 7 tries (29 points) to 2 goals (4 pnints)—about the biggest licking Bradford had ever had from Warrington. Watkin played an improved game with Taylor's coaching, and may yet distinguish himself in the ranks of the Cup-holders.

PERFECTLY CURED BY CUTICURA REMEDIES

"I have much pleasure in letting you know of the remarkable cure of my daughter of eczema. It was the worst case I had ever seen. To see her neck was a sickening spectacle, as you could see nothing but sores. We tried differ- ent kinds of ointments, but to no effect. We also had her under the care of two doctors for several weeks, ancj they gave us ointments and powders and ordered us to take off her ordinary clothes and keep on her night dresses, but to no avail, for where we applied the doctor's ointment it broke out worse. At last we gave up the medical men and tried a box of Cuticura Ointment, and after using part of same we could see a differ- ence, so we persevered with three more boxes of Cuticura Ointment. She was perfectly cured. Since then we have used Cuticura Soap and she has now a nice clean skin. P. Crowther, 62, Chell St., Longsight, May 29, 1906."

The proceedings at , the EDUCATION BILL annual meeting of the PROSPECTS. National Free Church

Council on Tuesday at Southport assure the Government of the cordial support of the Free Churches in their pending educational legislation There had been some doubt as to the attitude of a few prominent members of the Council regarding "contract- ing-out," but the resolution united all opinions, and Mr. MoKaNwe may now go forward knowing that be has at his back the great volume of Nonconformist opinion throughout the country. None of us like "contracting- out." It is forced on us by the unwillingness of the Church to accept any other solution, and it does at least leave a margin of cost to be provided by the denominations which may, in the future, be a legitimate outlet for denominational generosity and zeal. If, as the Congress desires, we can take steps to secure that there shall be no falling off in the efficiency of the schools which cut themselves off from rate-aid and local control, then, of course, the chief objection which has been urged by educationists against the principle will be disposed of. _ _

But the great thing welcomed on Tuesday was the freeing of the village schools from clerical control.To secure thatthere shall be a non-sectarian school within reach of every child whose parents desire that he should have a place in such school is worth some sacrifice, and if the " con- tracting out " of voluntary schools in areas where there is a choice of school is the price we have to pay for abolishing the sectarian monopoly in. the villages—well, the price must be paid Nobody ever suggested "contracting out " as an ideal solution of the, education problem. It has been arrived at by a process of exhaustion, an if the settlement now offered be rejected, we fear there is but the alternative of secular schools left. The amount being offered to the voluntary 'schools by the Bill is very large, much larger than I they were receiving in 1902, when they were saddled on the rates. It is no secret that the terms are proving attractive to the managers of such schools, for, with 475. per child, and the right to charge fees, they would be able to cover almost the whole cost of education without the necessity for subscriptions. There are two sets of forces, however, which will I have more effect in securing the passage of Mr. McKximi's Bill than either Nonconformist approval or denominational hostility. By the financial arrangements to be made when the Bill becomes law, substantial rate•relief will be given to local Education Authorities. In some cases this will be as high as 3d. in the 2. Local administra- tors, no matter whether they are Tory or Liberal, naturally view the possibility of a reduction of the local rate with much favour. In this particular, Mr. MoKnNteA has a greet advantage over Mr.Butastt. In 1906 Mr. BIRRILL proposed to give a million pounds, the bulk of which would have gone in rent to the voluntary schools. Mr. McismsA promises £1,400,000, every penny of which goes to relieve local rates. It is true that some of the extra money goes to the " contracted-out " schools, but they will cease to be a charge on the rates, so that really the whole of the extra mosey will go to the local authorities. In a few weeks we shall have the recognised Associations of County Council an' municipal administrators putting pressure on members of Parliament to prevent the wrecking of ithe Bill.

Then, the Tariff Reform Ca:npaign is all work- ing in favour of the Bill. It may he a coincidence, but it is nevertheless a fact that the high denomi- nationalists on the Opposition benches in the Commons happens at the same time to be free ooders, and they are becoming suspect with the rest of their party. The Tariff Reformers are beginning to think that those who desire to prevent a settlement of the Education problem have the ulterior motive of desiring to keep Tariff Reform in the background. Signs are la-coming evident that this will not long be allowed to continue, and it is well-known that those who are pushing Mr. CHAMBERLAIN'S propaganda would be glad to get the subject of Education settled and done with. Then they could deal " faithfully with Lord ROBERT CECIL and his friends." They now openly boast that they are strong enough in the Lords to prevent tie Bishops and the Free Fooders on the Opposition benches from wrecking the Bill in " another place." Altogether the prospects of something being accomplished are much brighter thma they were two years ago, and Mr. McKwww.t, knowing the position, must feel much encouraged in this task,

The Licensing Bench for Tam BRIDGE INN the Borough are to be con- LICENCE. gratulated on having stood

firm against the temptations and specious arguments offered to induce them to permit the transfer of the Bridge Inn licenca to a new public-house which Messrs. Green&ll, Whitley and Co. proposed to erect in the Gorsey-lane chs-

trict. They refused to grant the licence at the Brewster Sessions on February 7th last, and they refused again at the adjourned Sissions yester- day notwithstanding the "bargain " which was offered. Of course,theoretically,the Bench should be entirely unmoved by the seeming generosity of the brewers in offering to give up so much laud, and bricks and mortar, in addition to four oft- licences. The "real estate" is not for them but for the Corporation, though one magistrate did sug- gest that the value of it should be added to the compensation fund—an idea which seemed to find favour in the eyes of Mr. Rigby S miff- Evidently the brewers are not so much averse to surrendering licences for compensation as they would have us believe when " spoliation " seems a convenient adjective to use. But it is a temptation, neverthless, to a Licensing Bench of local public men to consider the material benefit to the town which may be contained in the brewers' barter, and they are entitled to the praise of all right thinking citizens when they say "No to the proposed exploitation of a new district by the Trade in exchange for public improvement facilities. For our own part we did not consider the offer made yesterday at all adequate, even were we disposed to look favouraNy upon the principle ot barter between Bench and brewer. The surrender of about three on-I icences in the Bridge-street area in addition to the off-licences, would more nearly approach a quid pro quo for a new licence in a growing district with little com- petition for the beer-seller. Oa the grounds of public welfare the transfer was most inexpedient, and we were glad to hear the argument so well stated by Mt. Bitty for the temperance party. The latter has too long been somnolent and the evidences of an awakening will be warmly welcomed in all quarters but those where self- interest clashes with the public weal. We should like to add hare that a meeting to organise the temperance forces in Warrington, and place them upon a workable basis, is to be held in the Winmarleigh Cafe on Monday evening. The watchword will be "to fight the Trade." All adherents to the cause may be sure of a cordial welcome!

The question what may THE PROGRESS happen in England and OF PROHIBITION. Wales at the end of four- teen years if the Licensing Bill be passed in its present shape is one that the Governinsnt and temperance reformers are dis- posed to let alone. All they want at present is to put a period to the vested interests of the brewers. It will be for the people to decide how many public-houses they have, ff any, and on what conditions. That there is a possibility of prohibi- tion in some areas cannot be denied ; but the English people, as a whole, have a keen dislike of any thing that favours of intolerance, and few persons seriously believe that this generation will See any of the English or Welsh licensing areas worked on the Main or Tennesse model, with need of getting alcohol from the chemists instead of openly. The curious thing is that, not- withstanding their protest that prohibition does not hurt their business, and the fact that internal revenue figures show ineressed sales of malt and spirituous liquor in 1907 over 1906, the brewers and liquor dealers are (sccording to " Collier's Weekly ") girding up their loins. Representatives of these interests from all parts of the country met the other day in Ky., to discuss the problems now confront• ing their business, and a number of papers describe their feelings as bordering upon panic. A move is being made by the Trade to wipe out the disreputable saloons. That is not remarkable. But it is worthy of note that the Trade is in the States showing signs of division. The following is from the "Detroit Journal : "The Mississippi Valley Brewers' Association, which has hitherto always combined with the whisky distillers to fight the prohibition movement, is c msidering the advisability of combining with the temper- ance men to fight the whisky traffic. They propose that whisky be altogether prohibited as a beverage and be accessible only as a medicine, to be bought at drug-stores and upon a doctor's certificate. In this way, they believe, the cause of temperance would he advanced." That would not do in Scotland.

NOTES AND NOTIONS.

We would remind our readers that the Infirmaay extension will be formally opened by Lord Cross on Thursday, March 19th. Particulars of the ceremony will be published next week. Since it came into power two years ago the Liberal Government has reduced the number of Chinese- labourers in South African mines by nearly half. Mr. Winston Churchill on Wednesday stated that the number on December 31st, 1905, was over 63,000, and on December 31st, 1907, over 35,000. * * * . _

The painters were in pos-session of the Board room at the Workhouse on Wednesday'and the Guardians had perforce to adjourn to an adjoining small room =tally used for the hearing of relief cases arising in the country parishes. The accommodation was so limited that seats could not be found for all, and a number of members had to stand throughout the pro- ceedings.

* * * The bold burglar who not long siAce attempted a raid on a Bold farm not only made painful acquaint- ance with a poker wielded by the doughty farmer but has now three years to spend in durance vile. It appears that other burglaries were traced to him and that he has been convicted previously of similar offences.

* * * At the Wigan Town Council on Wednesday Mr. Stoker (Socialist), speaking in support of the Small Holdings and Allotments Act, said that if they did not take the matter up it would be what the curate said. Mr. Parkinson (Labouf) : What did the curate say? Mr. Stoker: Why, "We must deal with the unemployed problem, or, by God, it will deal with us." (Laughter. The elective auditors for the ensuing year will again be Messrs. Bolton and England,who both headed Mr. W. Chrimes in the poll on Monday. It is surpris- ing how little interest is excited by this annual election. Out of nearly 12,000 electors in Warrington only 657 went to the poll. The cost to the borough was about £5O, or nearly is. 6d. per vote. * *

* . *

Now that the handsome and commodious 13o1ion School at Latchford is practically finished, the cottage property speculator has commenced to build along the front and one side. Before long it is probable the school will be surrounded by working class dwellings nestling as close to the walls as may be. The fatuity of it all is amazing. Surely the school could have been brought nearer the road and given a frontage worthy of the structure. Talk of Beautiful Warringon forsooth, why even the present generation cannot see the length of its eyelashes, apparently. ** * I Although nothing is heard nowadays of the arsenio, scare which greatly perturbed beer-drinkers a few years ago, the health authorities of Warrington do not ,* neglect to take precautions. There is, however, nol cause for alarm on that score at present. Mr. Rad- I dock, the borough analyst, had 25 samples submitted 1 Ito him last year and in his report he states that in two cases the beer was absolutely free from arsenic, but in all the other samples slight traces were persent up to .005 of a grain per gallon. This however, is only ts i 1 minute quantity and is harmless. His object n i mentioning it is to point out that the raw materials used in brewing are not as free from arsenic as they were immediately after the arsenio scare, and that it is as well for the brewers to watch this carefully. * * * ___

The good of mankind means the attainment by every man of all the happiness which he can ehjoy without diminishing the happiness of his fellowmen,— Huxley. Wisdom preaches temperance, not mortification 1 Seneca. Woman is an overgrown child that one amuses with toys, intoxicates with flattery, and seduces with promises. —Sophie Arnould. Discretion generally means having a good memort for the lies you have told.—John (Wirer Hobbes. An unmarried man is an untested man —in most cases a shirker of responsibilities.—Mrs. Craigie. Gossip is a sort of smoke that comes from the dirty tobacco pipes of those who diffuse it : it proves nothing but the bad taste of the smoker.—George Elio

Brevity is the soul of wit. Our worthy Mayor - evidently believes in the poet's dictum and in endea- vouring to get through the business of the Council in reasonable time on Tuesday amused the Council by the abrupt way in which he called upon some of the members to move the adoption of the minutes. For instance, he called out "Alderman Pierpoint, gas !" and the Council roared. The alderman might, of coarse, have construed this into an invitatlat to discourse, but he fell in with the spirit of brevity and contented himself by formally moving the minutes of the Gas Committee. * * There is a powerful influence that will operate in favour of the acceptance of the Education Bill. This is the relief which will be given to the ratepayers, t and I understand (says the London correspondent cf the " Liverpool Post and Mercury ") that this con- sideration is already beginning to exercise a consider- fir able effect on local bodies. The increased Privy Council grants, amounting altogether to ..,:1,400,000, which are to be given to all elementary schools, will save that sum to the ratepayers. The relief is sub- stantial. It will vary in different localities, but will not be less than a penny in the pound on the rates, and will in many cases be nearer than twopence. This fact is producing a very great effect on the local municipal authorities, and it is not the measure of the whole advantage that will be conferred on the rate- payers. Every school that contracts out —and there will be at least some thousands of them —will cease to have any assistance from the rates, and will, there- fore, reduce the charge upon them. No doubt this was one of the considerations which influenced the Education Minister when he handled his Education Ili Bill so skilfully.

I Platelayer Injured. On Monday night John Clare, a platelayer on the L. and N..W. Railway, and residing at Wilderspool Causeway was admitted to the Wart ington Infirmary with his arm almost severed from the shoulder. He met with the accident whilst working on the line. • Personal. Alderman Ellison has been confined to his bed- room for the past five weeks with an attack of bron- chitis. We are pleased to state, however, that this Week there has been a great improvement in his condition and he hopes shortly to be able to resume his public duties. Mr. Septimus Kenyon, so long known as a War- rington tradesman, has gone to spend his retirement at Rihbleton, Preston. He and his wife will bear with them the good wishes of a large circle of friends in Warrington and district. The Rev. Harry Wall is, we are pleased to state, making steady progress towards recovery from his severe attack of rheumatic fever. TRY GILLETTE SAFETY RAZORS .for comfort in shaving.—Agent, J. S. RURAL DISTRICT COUNCIL.

I The fortnightly meeting of this Council was held at Dutton Workhouse on Tuesday, Mr. G. Pilling in the chair. There were also present :—Messrs. J. Hallows, C. Roylance, J. Balshaw, E. Britoil, A. H. Talbot, P. Mountield, G.- Egerton Warburton, J. Whitlow, T. ' Baxter, P. Booth, C. E. Linaker, son., T. Wright, H. Mawdsley, T. Guest, S. Woodward, W. Harrison, J. L. Jones, E. Brethertont R. Nixon, J. Spruce, J. , Renshaw, W. Winstauley, C. E. Linaker, jun., W. Weir. , J. B. Chadwick, A. Haining, T. Antwis, J. Farrington, (inspector), J. Swinton, W. Diggle (surveyors). The Clerk read a letter from Mrs. W. E. Gorton, Grappenhall Lodge, Grappenhall,complaining about the state of the roads in the vicinity which she described as ! disgraceful and dangerous for horses and not fit for any carriage to travel over. About a month ago she wrote to the Surveyor about the road between Grappenhall village and Appleton, and was informed that it would have his attention but there was no improvement. She also called attention to the unsatisfactory condition of Cartridge-lane and asked for treatment of all the roads . by the steam roller. The Surveyor was instructed to attend to the matter. I 1 Vith regard to the appointment of an Alkali Inspector for the districts outside Liverpool and Runcorn, the Town Clerk of Liverpool reported that the conference of representatives of local authorities recommend such an appointment at a salary of £5OO per annum. The contribution of Runcorn Rural District , Council would be £25 per annum. ; The matter was agreed to. Mr. C. E. Linaker said Norley had not been repre- sented on the Council-for some time, and it seemed to , him that now the important work in connection with ; Norley was being carried on, the ratepayers should be represented. lie moved that the seat be declared vacant. Mr. Jones seconded, and the motion was carried.

BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

The fortnightly meeting of this Board was held at Dutton Workhouse on Tuesday, Mr. T. Baxter in the chair. There were also present :—Messrs. C. E. Linaker, C. E. Linker, jun., .1. Spruce, G. Pilling, J. Mawdsley, T. Wright, W. M. Morris, J. Hallows, J. L. Jones, C. ltoylance, W. R. Norman, J. Bolshaw, A. H. Talbot, E. Britch, P. Moundeld, G. E. Warburton, J. Whitlow, P. Booth, T. Guest, S. Woodward, W. Harrison, E. Bretherton, R. Nixon, J. Renshaa, W. ‘Vinstanley, W. Weir, J. B. Chadwick, A. Haining, G. F. Ashton (clerk), and the relieving officers. The Royal Commission on the Poor Laws, through Mr. R. G. Duff, the secretary, wrote stating that it had been represented to them that Boards of Guardians sometimes found difficulty in apprenticing young persons to skilled trades under the conditions laid down by the existing orders relating to apprenticeship and also that the desirability of pro- longing the period during which the Guardians may exercise parental control in these and other cases had led to increasing use being made of the statutory pro- vision for the adoption of children by the Guardians. The Commission applied for the views of the Runcorn Board as to any alteration of the law or practice on these subjects: Mr. Morris : I think it is about time the Parliamen- tary Committee was re-resurrected, Mr. Woodward : Re-resurrected ? (Laughter.) . .

Mr. Spruce seconded and the motion was carried. It was moved, seconded and carried that Messrs. Baxter, Spruce, Linaker, Jones, Morris, Woodward, Pilling, Davies, and the clerk be appointed a committee to consider the applications for the posts of master and matron, which numbered 70. Messrs. Harrison Mynekyme and the clerk, who attended the recent central Poor Law Conference pre- seuted a report of the proceedings which was adopted, the delegates being thanked. A letter was read from the girl's industrial trainer, Miss Jessie Clare, asking that her salary be increased as she bad been there nearly eighteen months. Mr. Morris moved that the matter be referred to the House Committee. Mr. Hallows seconded and the motion was carried.

ST. HELENS.

DEATH OF A ST. HELENS TRADES COUNCIL OFFICIAL.

The death occurred on Sunday of Mr. Augustine Craig at his residence in Raglan-street, St. Helens. He had been employed for many years in the printing trade, and was secretary of the sub-branch at St. Helens of the Liverpool Typographical Society, on the committee of which he had rendered much valued service, and in conjunction with the secretary (Mr. George Nelson) was largely instrumental in bringing the offices of the town into lino with the society. He was treasurer of the St. Helens Trades and Labour Council, and a prominent Oddfellow. Mr. Craig was also one of the pioneers and a present officer of the St. Helens Medical Aid Society. CONSERVATIVE CANDIDATE CHOSEN. ROYAL COURT THEATRE.

Mr. Norman V. Norman in Repertoire.

The visit of Mr. Norman V. Norman and his talented company this week has been quite a notable feature among local entertainments. Mr. Norman has an attractive repertoire of plays with which he is touring, and chief among these is " The Adventure of Lady Ursula," by thht talented and witty writer Mr. Anthony Hope. Lady Ursula Barrington is somewhst of a madcap as well as a very fascinating personality, and one of tier escapades lands to' or three people into trouble. First and foremost her brother, the Earl of Hassenden, has to challenge the redoubt- able Sir George Silvester to a duel—which, however, never takes place owing to further amusing complications caused by the wilful Ursula's attempt to undo the mischief she has made. Sir George is a woman hater who has barred his other- wise hospitable doors to the weaker sex, but Lady. Ursula obtains entry in the guise of her young brother, and in his garments too. There we might leave her. The scene is very amusing, but even less so than the one in which she has to face the music before a group of Hassenden's brother officers and maintain her role. Here the one dramatic note is struck, and there is a tense moment while the life ,of either herself or Sir George appears to hang in the balance. Finally, all ends well, and the woman-hater succumbs to the charms of Lady Ursula. In the title role Miss Beatrice Wilson acts with great spirit and betwitching drollery. Her representation of the masculine gallant is capital, and the interview with Sir George is splendidly done. Mr Norman V. Norman is an accomplished actor of fine presence, and he dominates the scene with true ' power when on the stage. Mr. G. T. Norman-Burt also acted with grace and, distinction as 'the aristo- cratic and supercilious Mr. Dent, while a clever study of the meek and reverend Mr.Brimboe is submitted by Mr. Sam Wilkinson. Miss Vera George makes a vivacious Dorothy Fenton, the bosom-friend of Ursula, and there is vigour in every line of Mr. Frederick Look's I work as the Earl of Hassenden. We should like to bave seen more of the Company in "Mr. Sheridan " last night but other engagements forbade. To-night the famous play written round "David Garrick" will be presented, with Mr. Norman in the title role. " David Garrick" will be preceded, for the first time in Warrington, by "A Privy Council," which was produced at the Haymarket Theatre, London, and ran for over twelve months, and was then revived at the same Theatre for a further run of four months. By special arrangement with Frederick .!arrison, Esq , a command performance of this play was given before His Majesty the King, at Sandring- ham on the occasion of his birthday, 9th November, 1905. The part of "Mistress Knipp " (of the King's Playhouse —Charles ) will be played by Miss Beatrice Wilson, and Mr. George Norman-Burt will play " Samuel Pepys "—the author of the celebrated Diary. 1

BLIND TYPIST.

The appeal for the Lambeth blind girl who is an expert typist and shorthand writer has met with success a large firm having given her employment.

EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON.

PRIMITIVE A.F.C. TEA PARTY.

The Earlestown Primitive A.F.C. held their annual tea party and social in the Schoolroom, Lord-street, last Thursday. About 100 people sat down to an excellent tea, the after proceedings taking the form of games, recitations and singing, a most enjoyable evening being spent. DEATH OF MRS. FARAGHER. We regret to announce the death of Mrs. Faragher, wife of Mr. James Faragher, of 18, Paterson-street, Earlestown. The deceased lady was highly respected in the district,, being a prominent member all her life, of the Wesleyan Church, and always to the fore in any work connected with it. ABour A SWEETHEART.

William Walters, Go['porno, was fined 40s. and costs for assaulting Ann Louisa Jones at Golborne on February ISth.—Complainant said when she was return- ing home from work with two companions, defendant struck her in the mouth, and kicked her. She bad had a quarrel with defendant's sweetheart, and she thought tkat was why the assault was committed.—Defendant said complainant attempted to strike him with a can first.

NEWTON TENNIS CLUB. The members of the Newton Tennis Club held their annual dance and whist drive in the Town Hall, Newton, last week, when aboutl3o members and friends attended. The object of the social was to assist the pavilion fund. Messrs. H. Walton and H. Owen acted as N.C.'s., and Mr. McLay was the manager of the whist party. The winners of the whist drive were Mrs. .1. White and Sir. W. Carter, Mrs. Derbyshire and Mr. W. H. Hindley. The booby winners were Miss E. Critchley and Mr. Fowler. Mrs. A Smith presented the prizes. A vote of thanks was accorded Mrs. A. Smith and the donors of the prizes. AIR RIFLE SHOOTING.
