161 I\Ljority for the School, 32. MOTORISTS SUMMONED.

John Thomas Parker, Louisa-street, Bolton, was summoned at Newton Sessions for driving a motor car and having no rear light attached at 10.33 p.m. on February sth in Church-street, Newton. P.C. Wilkes proved the case. Defendant said he stopped his car before he reached the constable. Wilkes stated that defendant was in company with another man on a motor cycle, and he whistled to defendant to stop. The case was dismissed.—William Slee Wadsworth, Bury New- road, Bolton, who was in company with the last defen- dant, was fined 10s. including costs for riding a motor cycle without a light at the same time and place. Defendant said he did not know the light was out. A FAMILY " JAR " AT GOLBORNE. Thomas Spibey, John Spibey, and Harriet Jones (brothers and sister), all of Golborne, were summoned at Newton Petty Sessions for creating a breach of the peace by fighting at Golborne on February 14th.—P.C. Potts said that at 10.20 on the night in question the defendants were fighting in Church-street, Golborne. They were bound over to keep the peace for six months in their own recognizances of £5 each.—Thomas Spibey was than summoned for assaulting Harriet Jones on the same date.—Mr. H. R. Rigby, Golborne, appeared for defendant.—Complainant said she was coming out of a pawnshop, when defendant and his wife were passing. The latter made a remark about her coming from the shop, and later on defendant came to her house, lie struck her in the side and in the mouth and knocked her down twice. In answer to Mr. Rigby, she said she had a grudge against defend- ant's wife. Mr. Rigby said when defendant and his wife were passing the pawnshop, complainant began to " call " defendant's wife. When defendant was coming home from the market, he called at complainant's house and asked for an explanation. Instead of an explanation given to him, abusive language was used, and com- plainant and her brother got hold of defendant and threw him out of the house. Complainant put her finger in defendant's mouth, and said she would pull his cheek out. Defendant did his best to get away from them and he did strike complainant. The constable, no doubt attracted by the noise, came upon the scene, and charged all three with commiting a breach of the peace. Having regard to all circumstances, he thought defend- ant was justified in doing what he did. Defendant and his wife, Jane Spibey, gave evidence, and the case was dis missed.—Harriet Jones was then summoned for assault- ing Jane Spibey, wife of the last defendant, on the same date. Mr. Rigby appeared for the complainant and said after the episode about which they had just beard, complainant went to the Red Lion Inn to wait for her husband. She went into the parlour, and while she was sitting there, defendant came in, in a wild manner, struck her violently and dragged her from one end of the room to the other. The landlord took defendant away, and handed her to Constable Potts, who took her home. Complainant and George Arthur Elliott the landlord of the inn, bore out I\lr. Rigby's statement. P.C. Potts also gave evidence. Defendant pleaded guilty, and was fined 20s. including costs.

MOTOR RECORDS AT BROOIMANDS.

A remarkable series of records was acco:nplished at Brooklitnds on ruesday by a group of Humber cars. There have been many records accomplished at Brook- lands, but always with either specially built racing cars or by what are technically known as" stripped " cars that is, chassis from which all the ordinary body has been taken off and in other ways specially prepared for the test. In this instance, however, there was no such special preparation, each of the Humbers being ordinary standard cars, with complete bodies, standard engines and gearboxes, and indeed exactly as sold to the public. The performance, therefore, affords the first genuine guide of what can actually be accomplishes' by a standard car on such a track as Brooklands. fhe team consisted of the principal makes of Humber:l at Coventry. There was the 10-12 h.p. Coventry Hutuber, ' sold at £235, the 15 h.p. Coventry Humber (£315) and the 30 h.p. six-cylinder Coventry Humber (1450). The times of each of these as officially certified were as follows : .

10-12 h.p. Coventry Humber 571• miles per hour. 15 h.p. do. 46; do. 30 h.p. do. 60 do. The most marvellous performance is that of the 10-12 h.p. Coventry Humber. That a car of such a low nominal horse-powey, and selling at only £235 should accomplish a speed of 371 miles per hour, with a standard chassis and body, shows the vast capabilities of the engine and the car. The performances of the other models were equally noteworthy and amply prove that , speed is equally a Humber characteristic in common with the well known reliability and everlasting wear of the most popular cars on the road. And again it should be noted that these performances are by cars exactly as sold to the public, so that the certified test may be taken as a guarantee by every buyer and user of the famous Humber productions, every item in which it should be noted is of purely British manufacture. In short, a veritable triumph for the British motor manufacture.

Insanitary Property. The executors of Benjamin Looker were summoned at the Borough Police Court on Monday under the Housing of Working Classes Act to show cause why a closing order should not be made in respect of 42 and 44, James-street, of which they are the owners. Mr. Steel, who represented the defendants, said the case was before the Court a month ago, when it was adjourned until that day to see if arrangements could be made between the Medical Officer of Health and the owners. No arrangements, however, had been made, as they had been unable to satisfy the authorities. A consider- able amount of work had to be done in connection with those houses, but unfortunately the owners bad not the money at present to do it. Therefore for the moment lie had no alternative but to consent to an order, and he would arrange with the Medical Officer that if at some future time they put the property in a proper state they could come before the Court and ask for the order to be annulled.—The Bench made all order for the property to be closed.

THE VOICE OF LABOUR.

Edited by John Workman. M.S.S. must be addressed to John Workman " Examiner " 05ce, Warrington and should be sent in not later than Wednesday in each week. Readers of this column are invited to assist in making it interesting and helpful to social workers by contributing themselves. Correspondents must write on one side of the paper only, and endeavour to make their letters and notes short and to the point. A nom-de-plume may be used, but the real ?tante of the writer should be forwarded in confidence to the Editor of the column. TEMTRANCE REF'ORNI. While in full sympathy with much that your able contributor " Demos " writes in your last issue, it is 'apparent he looks upon the United Kingdom Alliance as composed solely of teetotallers. This is not necessarily so as the appended extract from its " Declaration of principles clearly shows. " The Alliance has no test of membership bearing upon the personal habits of its members, or as to their religious creed or political party. It invites the aid and co-operation of all good citizens, whether abstainers or not."

- JAMES DODDS.

March 4th, 1008. THE CHILDREN'S CHARTER. The Education (Administrative Provisions) Act of last year in conjunction with the Feeding of School Children Act may be called " the children's charter." The powers of these Acts are very wide, and if the full advantage is taken of them by the local authori- ties they will bring about a great improvement in the whole environment of the children, with a subsequent improvement in their physical conditions, and in the health of the nation. Clause 1 of the Memorandum issued by the Board of Education on the question of medical inspection reads :—" The Education (Aminis- trative Provisions) Act, 1907, in so far as it concerns the medical inspection of school children, is the out- come of a steady movement of public opinion through- out the entire community. For some years years past evidence has been accumulating that there exists in certain classes of the English people a somewhat high degree of physical unfitness which calls for ameliora- tion, and, as far possible, for prevention. The Legislature resolved that to grapple effectively with this problem, or at least part of it, it was necessary first to improve the health conditions, both personal and in regard to environment, of the children of the nation. A consideration of the gravity of the need led to the conclusion that medical inspection of school children is not only reasonable but necessary as a first practical step towards remedy. Without such inspection we not only lack data, but we fail to begin at the beginning in any measure of reform. The reasonableness of such inspection, if it is conducted on sensible lines leading to an improvement of the surroundings and physical life of the children, must become evident bath to their parents and to the nation as a whole. The broad requirements of a healthy life are comparatively few and elementary, but they are essential,and should not be regarded as applicable only to the case of the rich."

The Board have decided that not less than three inspections during the school life of the child will be necessary to secure the desired results. The first inspection should take place at the time of, or as soon after, admission to school ; the second at about the third year, and the third at or about the sixth year of school life (say at the tenth year of age). It i. also recommended that a further iospection should take place immediately before the departure of the child into working life. The Act empowers the Board of Education to instruct local authorities to make additional inspec- tions and of a mole searching character in special areas if thought advisable by the Board. The inspec- tion of school children is to be the medium whereby the conditions of the home life and the environment of the people are t ) be ascertained. The Memorandum points out that the Act is not only for amelioration of the present undesirable conditions, but is meant to be preventive also. _

The Act empowers local authorities to provide vaca— tion classes, play centres,and many other things of such a charae.ter which may be considere I to be in the interest of the physical improvement of the children attending public elementary schools. Children which are found to be defective in any way will be subject to more careful inspection than the average child. In the inspection notice must be taken of : (a) height and weight ; (b) nutrition (underfeeding or improper feed- ing) ; (c) cleanliness (iacluding vermin of head and body) ; (d) clothing (sufficiently, cleanliness and foot- gea.r.) . . .

From the foregoing it will he seen that the Act will be a most valuable one, and goes a long way towards what Socialists have advocated for some years i.e. the State maintenance of school children. If food and decent shelter must be provided, why not clothing? There are many other points which space forbids me to mention. In conclusion I trust that the whole- hearted support of the teachers and parents will be given to assist in carrying out the Act so that every possible good may be obtained from it. There can be no going back.

The Examiner, Saturday March 7, 1908.

MY DEAR CHILDREN, How very busy I have been this week in goifig through the various suggestions sent by my members for new competitions. Some are very excellent, and will no doubt be utilised in the future. Now, you must bear in mind that I am a very modest man, and don't like talk- ing about myeelf. Well, quite a lot of letters have come to hand asking me to arrange a little drawing competition, and the subject to he—myself. Well, if you wish it I will submit. During the next four weeks this competition will be open—" the best sketch received of Uncle Ned." Send all efforts drawn on post- cards. I am not going to tell you what I am like, but I shall be very curious to see your ideas. One word, though, please, don't make me too ugly! The competition, as announced last week, must not be forgotten—the best penny toy sent in to be given to poor children. Fortull_particulars see my letter to you of last

__ LE "Bill," said a country parson, "I want a. little talk with you." Bill composed himself comfortably, and snuggled down on his stool. "Cleanliness, Bill," began the parson, "is one of the cardinal virtues of man." " Wot I " exclaimed Bill. " Don't I wash'?"

"Yes, Bill, you do wash, and, on the whole, you are a moderately cleanly personae; but you don't wanh all over. I am continually re- minded whenever I see you of the fact that you neglect your upper lip." "That ain't dirt, parson," objected the man; "that's my moustache!" "Wll, then, why don't you shave?" "Well, ye see," announced Bill, "a man's gotter have some place to strike his matches on." GERALD APPLEBY.

UNCLE NED'S DREAM.

Uncle Ned sat in his room, busily engaged in preparing the " Children's Corner" for the next publication. ".1..h: done at last," he murmured, as he drowsily leaned back in his chair. He sat gazing at the clock with ite. monotonous " tick- tcck," till at last he fell asleep. Uncle Ned woke up with a start; he had been dreaming. r,He then put on his coat and hurried home to tea. While at tea he told his peculiar dream. _ _

"I was in my office, when I heard a little tap-tap' on my door, and a little squeaky voice saying. • Uncle Ned, are you there?' I went to the door, and saw a poor little girl with hardly any shoes to her feet, and a ragged old shawl on her head. I took her in, and asked her some questions. 'Well, what brings you here, Minnie?' for ivkich was her name. 'Father got chunk, and I ain't had anything to eat all day.' 'You must be very hungry then,' I answered. I then gave her some buns, four of which she eagerly devoured. After a time, she said, 'Uncle Ned, could I take these two buns home to mother?" Certainly,' I replied; but I could not help thinking of the warm little heart under these rags. Though only a dream, I hope the readers of the Children's Corner' will copy Minnie's example, and thus fulfil one of God's holy Commandments."

CRAVED FOR LOVE.

"Is is true that you threw something at your husband that eaused the swelling over his eye? " the county magistrate inquired of a little wiry Iriehwoman, who appeared one day, sobbing as if her heart would break. "Yis, sr, I did," said the woman, catching her breath "but I niver mint to hurt him, an' well he knows it. We'd just come home from me cousin's weddin', an' I was feelin' kind o' soft to Mike, an' I up an' axed him if he loved me as much as he did the day we was married; an'—an' yer 'anner, he was slow antswerin' me, that. I up wid the mop an' flung it at him. If we poor women don't have love our hearts jist breaks inside av us." LILY ROSE.

Dear Uncle Ned,—l was very pleased to find I had won eeeond prize in " Clothing " com- petition. I must thank you very much for the nice box of chocolates which I received yester- day. I had no idea I should win such a nice prize. I seem to have been in luck's way lately, us my twelfth birthday was on January 31st, when I had no less than twenty-one presents, amongst which were five books. I am very fond of books, and they are my dada's " hobby." He has hundreds. He was telling me not long ago that he met you in the train, and you may be sure that I asked him plenty of questions about you; but he does not care to tell me all he knows, but I hope I may see you some day. I am enclosing a little piece of poetry, which I have made up myself on a little tale I heard. DOROTHY M. STARKE.

A gentleman was fishing recently from Brighton pier-head. He had been unsuccessful for the last two hours, when one of the local idlers, who had been watching him closely, hazarded this remark, "I , say, sir, what bait have you got on yer 'ook?" "Cheese," said the angler, who knew more of fresh water angling than salt. "Why, bless yer," said the man, "you've come to the wrong place; there ain't any mice here I" GERTIE SCRIVIEN.

LITTLE THINGS. Line by line the book is written, , Stitch by stiat the pattern groweth, Stone by stone the temple riseth, Drop by drop the etrearnlet floweth. Drops and lines and stones and stitches, Tiny things becoming grand; Lay ye to your hearts the lesson, Travellers to the far-off land. Faint not, for the goal is certain,

- Step by step ye draw .anear, Day by day sure progress making Towards the home ye hold so dear.

A man who has travelled round the world has discovered as the result this remarkable fact: "A young Jones is born every forty minutes."

"What curious things carpets are " said a girllittle to her mother. "Why so?" asked her mother. " Because, although they are bought by the yard they are worn out by the feet."

He paused for a reply, when the little pedlar eqeaked out, "No. sur ; I comes to dis country to sell cheap ready-made clothe."

THE CRAFTY IRISHMAN.

An Englishman, Irishman, and a Scotchman once had. a big loaf given to them to divide. Not knowing,how to do this, they decided to go to sleep, and the one that had the biggest dream •should have the loaf. So when they awoke, the Scotehman said to the Englishman, "What did you dream?" "I," said the Englishman, "dreamed that all London belonged to me." Then the Englishman said to the Scotehman, "What did you dream?" "I," said the Scotchman, "dreamed all the world belonged to me." Men the Englishman said to the Irishman, "What did You dream?" "I," said the Irishman, "dreamed I was hungry, so I got up and eat the loaf." _

FLOSSIE C. NE,LLZ.

PONDERING OVER A PROBLEM.

===== 0002601_19080307_art0074.txt | included=true | included =====
GFOOTBALLS VEve

"I've been pondering over a, problem of life," said A. "What is it?" said B. "How putting a ring on a woman's third finger should place you under the woman's thumb." CYRIL CARMELL.

HE KNEW. In a quiet little village, Far from any market town, Lived a simple country yokel, Whose name was Thomas Brown. Re really was quite silly, The other lads would say; He never did a stroke of work, Or helped in any way. He did not know a shilling From a sovereign made of gold; He knew no worth in money, At least, so they'd been told. One day they thought they'd test him, So Farmer Stokes's son Called the village lads together, And planned to have some fun. Let's lay a golden sovereign And a shilling on my hand, Just to se which one he chooses, Said the leader of the band. So they called poor Tom one morning, And did as Stokes had told; " Take which you like, Tom, lad," said he. They feared he'd take the gold. But, no, Tom took the shilling, Their amazement knew no bound; To think that Tom had been so green As to miss a golden pound. This trick was tried by others, But Tom always did the same; They thought him very silly, But still enjoyed the game. One day Tom's mother asked him Why the shilling he always took, And why he refused the sovereign— He gave her a knowing look. Torn answered her quite coolly, "Why, you silly, can't you see, I always take the shilling 'Cause its much more use to me I" "I always take the white 'un; It's more to me, I'm sure, * For if I took the gold 'un, I'd never get no more."

, DOROTHY STARKE

A schoolmaster who happened to have red hair was giving an object lesson to a class on nutt3.

In his endeavour to draw a distinction be- tween a hazel nut and other nuts, he held one up. to. view,. asking:— _ .

"What kind of -a nut is this?" at the same time, without a thought, putting his pencil to his head while waiting for an answer. Suddenly a young chap, who was noted fpr his witty answers, replied, "A ginger nut, sii." F. R. KNOWLES.

Earwigs, though they are not devoid of good qualities, must be reckoned as destructive in- sects, doing much harm in gardens by the injury they-cause to flowers and fruit. Could we credit all the storieS about them we might believe that, not content with injuring man's property, they even attack himself, as they are supposed to enter the ears of sleeping persons and penetrate the brain. This story, which has apparently no foundation in fact, has ex4sted in all times, and in all countriee. In Britain. there are several 'kinds of earwigs, some larger and some smaller than the common earwig, but they are -rarely seen. The largest of the European species, nearly twice the size of the common one, lives amongst the stones on the seashore and on the banks of the riiiers, feeding on insects and other invertebrate animals.

Three gentlemen during a converoation agreed to pay a guinea each to the one who should tell the tallest and most ridiculous story. The Ant commenced his story thus: " There was once an honest lawyer*" " Stop!" cried the rest of the party, '"here's our money."

FREE CHURCH COUNCIL.

PUBLIC MEETING AT WARRINGTON.

Vigorous Speeches.

In connection with the annual gatherings of the i National Free Church Council which have been held at Southport this week, a public meeting promoted by the Warrington and District Free Church Council was held in Bold-street Wesleyan Church on Thursday evening. There was a large and representative attendance and the powerful speeches which wore delivered were listened to with grelt interest. Mr. C. E. Parker presided in the absence of Mr. Moses Atkinson, of Leeds, who had been announced to take the chair, and there were also present on the platform the Revs. James Travis (Chester), J. G. Stevenson (Brighton), W. Kingscote-Greenland, (Chingford), S. Pitt, T. Miller, John Yonge, J. 13. Buglass, J. S. Hughes, and Mr. E. Wynes Jones (one of the hou. secretaries). Mr. Wynne Jones read a litter of apology far non- attendance from Mr. Moses Atkinson. THE EDUCATION QUBSTION.

The CHAIRMAN said that as they had such all excel- lent deputation he did not intend to give anything in the nature of an address. He would have liked to refer to the education question because he thought they had been too silent in Warrington with regard to it. (Hear, hear.) The other aspects of the question had been brought into such prominence and the citzen's aspect had been ignored and those representing the citzens had been silent. Possibly other opportunities would be open for him to say what little he could upon that subject.

===== 0002601_19080307_art0076.txt | included=true | included =====
THE NEEDS OF THE AGE.

The Rev. W. KINGSCOTE-GREENLAND, who is well known as the editor of the" Young Man," began his address by announcing that he was a Methodist preacher who devoted a third of his time to the Baptist denomination and a third to the Congregationalists— (langhter)—so that he was a pretty good living illustra- tion of the federated churches. Personally he had no denominationalism in him, and there were few things he had less interest in and less respect for than sectarianism. He did not care the dust on their linoleum what was the name of the chapel or the denomination he was in or speaking for. He gave them the same gospel and very often the same sermon. (Laughter.) Alluding to the fact that the National Council had given unanimous support to

THE EDUCATION' RILL, he said they had not done so with any blind- ness to the very serious matter of the con- tracting-out clauses. He hoped no-one thought that their unanimity meant that they were per- fectly satisfied with what they had got. As not only Free Churchmen, but as something a million times more important—as present-day followers of Jesus Christ—they sent all the support and moral backing they could to the Licensing Bill. (Applause.) Mr. Greenland proceeded to address himself to the young people. One of the few bright signs of these very perplexing times was that there never were so many people in the world so earnestly desiring to bless the world with some gift for its good as there were to-day. He did not believe there were ever so many would-be philanthropists alive as to-day, but side by side with that fact there were the two pathetically qualifying ones that there was great perplexity as to what it was the world wanted to have done for it or to have given to it, and there was 'great uncertainity and diffidence on the part of young people as to whether, even if they knew what the world wanted, they had power to give it. Curiously enough, the idea of giving was becoming almost inextricably associated with the giving of material gifts, not to say of one material gift—money. Necessary as money was in its own limited place there was not a church in England to-day of any denomination whose

NEED OF MONEY was not its last need. People had the impression that, the world must be redeemed by the great and ' wealthy and by those who could give largely.l Thirty-nine millions out of the 43,00%000 t of us did not come within the beneficent clause of the income tax—(laughter)—so that really we were a nation of paupers with a few swells at the top. (Laughter.) If the world had to be redeemed by the great r,ifts of great people we could not redeem it and it would never be redeemed. Was there no higher philanthropy ? He thought there was. The speaker went on to show that the best gifts in life, the most operative and influential gifts, were gifts that money could not buy and which had no financial equivalent. God's greatest gift to everyone present of any ordinary char- acter, short of some supernatural gift or of some tragic exception, was the gift of their mother. In a thousand times more important and deeper sense than the merely physiological one, they were all their mother's children. If he were Mr. Carnegie or Baron Rothschild, which God forbid, and he wanted to do a service to them he could not base their lives at this day in an influential, deep, splendid and operative motherhood. The speaker also pointed out what a great deal of their moral stand- ing and of what they counted for in their day and generation they owed to things they bad read. If it were true that the gifts which had gone to their making were not purchasable with money, did it not suggest that probably the world was perishing for the want of some immaterial gift and if that could be estabished,the sphere of church philanthropy for those who had no great material gifts was opened up. At that very hour the world was perishing for the lack of four great gifts and If it possessed them it. would be saved. The first was the gift of dedicated, consecrated intelligence. Most people were willing to do anything for God's Kingdom but to think for it. Many who laid their money on God's altar would not put their intelligence, judgment, shrewdness and power of thought at His dis- posal. Referring to the unwillingness of large numbers of educated young people to become Sunday school teachers, Mr. Greenland asked what was the use of a good education if it only turned them into snobs? Alluding to the importance of Christian men devoting attention to

PUBLIC AFFAIRS, be said he beard all over the country how men of wisdom, integrity and strong religious position in the towns, quiet men, did not like the hurly-burly of municipal life, and the Councils fell into the hands of the sycophant, the jobber, the contract-hunter, and we got the scandals we were getting all over the country. It was a Christian duty to sit on the Town Council and watch the interests of one's fellow-citizens. The second great gift for which the world was dying was moral inspiration, and it was also desperately in need of comfort. This world was not a happy one, and they as Christians were the ambassadors and followers of One who was pre-eminently the great Consoler. The in of England, continued the speaker, was not immorality or drunkenness. How was it their ministers were always preaching on the sins of the flesh? Why did they not observe the *aster's own propor- tion and emphasise the sum of the spirit ? There were churches in all denominations which were in the grip of men of bitter and disturbing spirit and they let them go on year after year, to the death, stag- nation and crippling of their churches. There was something better than comfort which they could impart to the world and that was their religion. The only gift worth giving to God or man, concluded Mr. Greenland, the gift which both desired and needed was not the gift of what we had but the gift of what we were. The essence of Christianity was not self-sacrifice but self- expression. Let them pay their duty to God and men not only in the yellow currency of the realm, but as they loved England and their own denominations and as, for Christ's sake, they loved men and women, let them pay it in the blood red currency of their own hearts. (Applause.) THE RISE OF THE DEMOCRACY.

The Rev. J. G. STEVENSON, who succeeded the Rev. R. J. Campbell at Brighton, began by congratulating the meeting on having a man possessed of such public spirit and so thorough-going a Nonconformist as Mr. Parker for their chairman. (Applause.) The present time, he went on, was ripe with great issues end, if only for their own sakes, they must speak out. There was not the slightest doubt that at last the democracy were awake. Which way they were looking and to what things they were alert were perhaps for question, but there was the fact that for good or evil, as he believed for good, the democracy were absolutely awake. There must be in the political movements of the people of to-day a very strongly religious element. There was no safe base for the democratic theory except a spiritual base.lf the people were simply going to rule the people by stress of numbers or by united pimical force, then there was no continuity, no permanence in any Government that was so built up. The o ly possible way in which they were going to get RAL AND SOUND STATE was along these lines—the spiritual vision not only of the rights of the people to rule themselves but of their duties to rule. Only so far as in the up-building of a democracy they spiritualised every part of the fabric, were they going to build a temple that would last!. Since history was God's Bible for the nations, if history meant anything at all it meant that there must be no secular politics, especially in a democracy, and he bad no belief in any day of the people that was not heralded by the bright morning star that stood still over the Bethlehem stable. It was well for the people to have the oars, but woe to the people unless Christ was at the helm. From what quarter was that religious element to be supplied ? There were only two qunrters from which at present it was likely to come—from , their high Anglican and Roman Catholic friends or from churches of the type that were federated , in the Free Church Council. Whatever they could do I with John Bull, they could not drive him. They must I give him religious ideas that coalesced with national characteristics, and that type would ultimately provide the religious element in the national democracy. Mr. Stevenson sought to show that the Roman Catholic and High Anglican principles of religion were at variance with the national characteristics of individuality, courage and initiative. The ideal of Roman Catholicism and High Anglicanism was subordination, but the idea

_

of Protestantism and Free Churches was development, and that was at the bottom of

THE EDUCATION. QUESTION.

(Hear, hear.) It was not a matter of wrangling between Church and Chapel. The Bill which had put been brought in was not as their friends were trying to say, a Chapel Bill. Those contracting-out clauses would not • be in it if it were anything of the sort, and Cowper. Temple teaching was not dissenting teaching. Their friends said undenomlnational teaching was not Chris- tianity—that it was this, that and the other. He , had a little question he would like to ask ' them. What wds it that held the Church of England together to-day? 'Was it not the same t undenominational teaching that they said was not , 1 Christianity? Think of the divisions in the Church I lof England—Low Church, Broad Church and High Church,—.John Rensit, Lord Halifax, and Canon I , Hensley Henson. What roped those three together? ilt was not devotion to the establishment, it was not 'the love for the loaves and fishes that came from a I State connection. What held them together? Unde- • nominationalism, of course—the sort of sub-stratum of ' religion on which they all agreed. (Applause.) Cowper- , Temple teaching, as the present Archbiskop of Canter- bury said more than once, was a base di which they could build up later on either the teaching of the Church or any other teaching. This Bill was not Nonconformist Bill. It was a civic Bill—a Bill for making citizens, and it was only Nonconformist to this extent: The watchword of *Nonconformity and of Protestantism was development. The watchword of true citizenship was development for every individual to his maximum possibility, and that was the only extent to which it was a Nonconformist , Bill. Reverting to the matter of individuality .1 as exemplified in the Free Churches the speaker said there was nothing worse in the world than ; ; across-grained individuality,but there was nothing better I I than a consecrated individuality and by that consecrated , individuaility the Free Churches existed to-day. There ; were places in England to-day where it took plenty of pluck to be a Free Churchman. Their fathers made ' their churches what they were because they had the courage and initiative to come out of churches that were not worthy of them. Individuality, courage, initiative were all vital principles of Free 'Church life. Then for heaven's sake, let the democracy look for ; religious guidance in directions where they would get the type of religion that coalesced with the national ! genius and which would assist them on the right lines • to a God-given development. (Applause.) Mr. • I Stevenson concluded by emphasising that the spiritual counted for most of all, and the vital question was not , to which denomination or political party they belonged , or what they thought of tariff reform or anything of ti the sort. The vital question was : what was their , relationship to Christ, and how far were they denomin- ated by it? (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19080307_art0077.txt | included=true | included =====
NO CAUSE FOR PESSIIIISn.

The Rev. JAMES TRAVIS, of Chester, an ex-President of the Primitive Methodist Conference and of the National Free Church Council, was the last speaker. He said disparagement of the churches and their work was one of the most marked features of the day. Censures were hurled at them from pulpit, platform and Press,from club-room and public-house, from politicians and from Mr. Balfour's famous" man in the street." All

===== 0002601_19080307_art0078.txt | included=true | included =====
THB EVILS OF SOCIETY

were laid at the door Of the Church. That was some- 1 times done by politicians who would strive to enervate any measure for crippling the liquor traffic or for removing social evils which were a disgrace to our civilisation and an insult to our Christianity. (Applause.) The cry of the failure of the churches was as easy as it was fashionable. If the critics meant that the churches had not accomplished all they might or ought to have done,then headmitted failure. But apply that test all round, and where were they going to meet with success ? There was no Church, no political party, no charitable organization, no national institution which had accomplished all that it might have done. There was no preacher, no statesman, no teacher, no reformer, no leader of labour who had done all that he might have done. Our political and social reforms, our hospitals and infirmaries, our schools and colleges had not done all that they might have done by a long way, but they had done enough to repay us many a time over the time, energy and money we had expended. "If you could blot out the direct and the indirect work of the Christian Churches of this country you would paralyse and destroy our benevolent institutions; you would take the wheels from the chariot of modern progress and extinguish in the hearts of men the purest motives, the noblest aspirations and the most inspiring hopes that ever burnt in human souls. We have done enough surely to save us from being blighted by the sneer of the cynic or shrivelled up by the wail of the pessimist." (Applause.) Alluding to

===== 0002601_19080307_art0079.txt | included=true | included =====
FREE -CHURCH PROGRESS,

Mr. Travis said they were now in the majority in Englsnd etnd Wales. They provided far more religious accommodation and they had about 1,000,000 more children in their Sunday schools, and taking into account universal Christendom, their communicants were three, four or five times as numerous as the com- municants in the Anglican Church. The Free Churches had persistently resented privileged priesthood s and ecclesiastical castes, and they had stood for the divine right of every man, woman and child to free and direct access to God through Jesus Christ without the inter- vention of any priest, altar or ecclesiastical formulary. (Applause.) They must turn their faces to the future with unwavering faith. There never were so many capable ministers and laymen in their churches as there were to-day. Their Free Churches never had the wealth, intelligence, prestige, power, facilities and opportunities they had to-day, and with the spirit of their fathers burning like a fire in their bones, they would accomplish greater things than ever they had done. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19080307_art0080.txt | included=true | included =====
THE LICENSING BILL.

Mr. E. WYNN-E:JTON-is-i-nfe-r-ri;ea the Meeting that by Sunday next resolutions would be circulated in every Church and Sunday school on the Licensing Bill and it was hoped that they would take up the matter with all possible earnestness. They must back up the Govern- ment—(hear, hear).—for if they did not, who would ? the brewers, the House of Lords ?—certainly not. They had to fight the matter and he hoped they would do it bravely and well. He also suggested that as many Free Churchmen as possible should send postcards to the Borough Member reminding him of what he should do at this time. (Applause.) The meeting then closed with the Doxology.

CONCERT AT STOCKTON HEATH.

With the commendable object of assisting the funds I of the Stockton Heath District Nursing Association a concert, arranged by the Misses Sutton, was held in the Church Institute on Monday evening. It was gratifying to see the effort so welt supported by the inhabitants of the locality. Every available seat was occupied and the audience was appreciative and enthusiastic in a marked degree. The programe was of more than ordinary interest and was su agreeably diversified as to maintain keen attention for three hours. The Misses Sutton and pupils who were responsible for the major part of the entertainment are to be warmly congratulated upon the success of their enter- prise. With the majority of the audience, the most popular features were undoubtedly the costume songs and dances in which a number of young ladies and gentlemen took part. The bright and, tuneful "Tell me pretty maiden " from Florodora " was admirably given by the Misses Davenport, Nield, Walsh and Isherwood, and Messrs. L. Sutton, V. Roberts, J. Sutton and F. Isher- wood. The pretty coon songs and dances " Dinah " and "Swanee River " by the Misses Davenport, Nield, Rogers, Walsh, Isherwood and Dobson were particu- larly pleasing and a special word of praise is due to the soloist, Miss Davenport. The quartet and dance " Come little girl " in which the Misses Walsh, Nield and Davenport and Messrs. Isherwood, L. and J. 1 Sutton were associated, also went with a delightful swing, and the Spanish dance, with which the concert concluded, was gracefully executed. The attractive- ness of each of these items was enhanced by the lime- light effects, and the productions were adequate in every respect. Miss M. Sutton gave a pianoforte solo, Mendelssobn's "Andante " and " Rondo Capricciosa " in a distinctly creditable manner, evidencing good technique and tasteful expression. She was associated with Miss E. Isherwood in the duet "Polish dance" which was well played. Miss F. Sutton possesses vocal talents of a high order and her rich and powerful veice found full scope in "The -enchantress ' which was artistically interpreted. A hearty encore was responded to with " A May morning," affording a pleasing con- trast in styles. Other songs which added to the enjoyment of the evening were :—" Down the vale " by Mrs. Law, "Love's coronation " by Miss Roscoe (with violin obligato by Mrs. • W. B. Roberts), " Take a pair of sparkling eyes" and " The last watch " by Mr. E. Houghey (Manchester), " Asthore " by Miss B. Robertson, "Bedouin love sonerand " ! Jolly Jenkin " by Mr. Greenhalgh. Miss Robertson and Mr. Greenhalgh had the honour of recalls and as additional items sang " The Holy City " and " The Veteran's song " respectively. Mr. T. Boothroyd fully maintained his reputation as an accomplished violinist, his renderings of Sonatina "(Farmer), and "Chanson Polonaise " (Wieniawski) being. characterised by brilliance of execution and musicianly taste. He was vigorously encored for the latter and played the beautiful " Berceuse de Jocelyn " (Godard). The humorous part of the prograrna.e was in the hands of Mr. Victor Roberts whose songs and patter were quite up-to-date and created considerable merriment. Miss M. Sutton and Mrs. W. B. Roberts were efficient accompanist.

-- FUNERAL SERVICE IN A BALLROOM. In connection with the funeral of Lord Linlithgow on Thursday there was a very simple service in she ballroom of the residence of the deceased peer. The plain funeral car upon which the coffin was conveyed to ,the famity mausoleum was drawn by two bay horses.

MARCH COUGHS AND COLDS.

Quite an Epidemic of Chest Troubles.

The bitter frosts of a few weeks ago, followed by mild spring-like weather (mixed with occasional storms), have not only played sad havoc with the weak chested, but have strained to the utmost the powers of the most robust. The result is that at the present time there are more people suffering from chest colds, sore throat, bronchitis, and especially from a severe form of bronchial asthma, than for some time past. In every home a box of Peps should be kept always handy, and used promptly on the first sign of throat or chest trouble. Be it only a husky cough or a alight tickling in the throat, or a suspicion of pain or tightness about the chest, it is time to take a few Peps, which are a medicine quite unlike the old-fashioned lozenges and opium- laden mixtures, and many times more effective. Peps bring ease and strength to the throat, and the rare volatile fumes that are released when a tablet is gissolved on the tongue, convey a valnable medicine right to the uttexmost limits of the lungs and chest where ordinary medicine cannot pene- trate.

Peps are invaluable for coughs, colds, bron- chitis, and all throat and chest ailments. Peps give just the help Nature needs to fight against the perils of the sudden weather changes of Spring. Peps contain no dangerous drugs, such as are commonly used in liquid cough-mixtures and cheap imitations. Beware of imitations and see the name—Peps—on every Is. lid. and 2s. 9d. box.

Warburton and Sankey Bridges.

The monthly meeting of this Council was beld at Bewsey Chambers on Wednesday, Dr. Murray presiding. There were also present :—Messrs. ,R. Porshaw, A. Brooks, J. Backhouse, P. Longton, W. Furness, W. Houghton, H. H. Burnage, B. S. Newcombe (surveyor), Dr. Sephton (medical officer of health), F. W. Kerfoot (inspector). and 3.C. Sutton (clerk).

ROAD-REPAIR. AT MARTINSCROFT.

The CLERK read a letter from the Clerk of the Woolston with Martinscroft Parish Council (Mr. J. Daintith) asking the Rural District Council to repair the 'road along the south and east sides of Martinscroft Green such road being recognised as a public thorough- fare.

Mr. HOUGHTON said that the green in question was a recreation ground and the public used it as a short cut to tho other side of the green. The SURVEYOR said the road should certainly be rcpaired, and the work was ordered to be done.

===== 0002601_19080307_art0085.txt | included=true | included =====
RIXTON AND. WARBURTON BRIDGE.

The CLERK produced letters from the chief engineer of the Ship Canal Company stating that he had examined the bridge and the approaches thereto on the 3rd inst., and so far as he could see the flooring of the bridge was in the same condition as it had been for many years past.

Another letter stated that the engineer would be pleased to meet the Surveyor and the Surveyor of the Bucklow Rural District Council to discuss without prejudice a proposal made some time ago, that the Ship Canal Company should hand over the bridge and its approaches to the local authorities. The matter was left in the hands of the sub- committee.

A conference has recently been held in Liverpool for the consideration of the appointment of an additional Alkali Works Inspector, which Mr. Brooks and the Sur- veyor attended. The salary was fixed at £5OO, to which this Council would contribute £25.

Heath, and Shugais, forwards. . _ Referee, Mr. R. Robinson, Bradford. Touch judges. Mr. H. Lowe, Broughton, and Mr. F. Longden, Swinton Winning the toss Halifax took advantage of the slight breeze which' blew down the field, and had the sun at their backs. In the opening stages both aides pressed in turn, but on the whole the "wire-pullers " had the best of matters, and caused their opponents a good deal of anxiety. A clever combined movement by Fish and Dickenson aroused a good deal of enthusiasm. Fish started it, and Dickenson carried the ball over the line, but the referee ruled that the IVarringtoa wing , three-quarter had gone into touch, and the point was ' not allowed. Subsequently, when the game was in the home team's half,the Warringtonians were penalised for obstruction. The referee himself did not detect the fault, but the touch judge saw the informality, and acquainted him with it. An opportunity was thus given to Little, who kicked a fine goal. This gave more zest to the encounter. When Warrington were attacking, Ward intercepted a pass prettily, and was well nigh scoring. Little had another penalty shot at goal, but the ball passed just under the bar. The next feature was a dribble by Dickenson, which carried the game into the Halifax half, and threatened their line. Little, however, pulled him up just in the nick of time. Still Warrington pressed, and some keen pin was witnessed near the Halifax line, but the defence wet very sound. Just before the interval Halifax had a very uncomfortable time. A combined dribble by Heath, Thomas, and Belton carried the ball over the line' but Little managed to kick dead. Then Brooks got hold and passed to Dickenson, who threw to Fish, and the flyer dashed over amid great cheering. Thomas made a poor attempt at goal. A moment later, for off-, side' play, Warrington were subjected to the penalty rule. Little again took the kick, but the ball struck the cross-bar, and rebeunded into play. After this, Brooks passed to Dickenson, who romped over. Thomas' kick struck the post, and missed its object. At the interval Warrington were thus leading by 2 tries (6 points) to Halifax 1 goal (2 points). On change of ends IVarrington had, of course, the wind in their favour, and since they had established so useful a lead, their prospects of success looked very rosy. For fully ten minutes Halifax were kept busily on the defence. And then when the game was at half- way, Joe Riley charged down a kick by Taylor, and getting the ball at his toes beat every one of his opponents, and scored a really fine try. This Little converted, and Halifax were a point in front. So remarkable a change made the game more exciting, and both sides worked with grim determination. Tilley distinguished himself by a splendid dribble, which almost ended in a try, and Joe Riley ran half the length of the field, and had the mortification of seeing his pass mulled. After a series of struggles near the Halifax line, the ball was kicked to Watkin, who, how- ever missed a splendid openiag. Halifax were also unlucky in not adding to their score. Towards the ()lose Thomas' of Halifax, let in the opposition, and Jenkins scored behind the posts for Thomas to add the extra points. From this point the game was clearly Warrington's and at the close they had won a grim struggle by 1 goal, 3 tries (11 points), to Halifax 2 goals, 1 try (7 points).

THE NORTHERN RUGBY UNION. Warrington Swamp. Bradford.

The return match between Warrington and Bradford was played at Wilderspool on Tuesday before 3,000 spectators. Bradford were short of three of their best back., Connell, Davidson and O'Nedl,and the substitutes proving weak Warrington had an easy victory. Teams:— Warrington— rilley, back ; Fish, Dickenson, Taylor, and Watkins, three-quarter backs ; Lloyd and Brooks, half backs ; Shugars, Heath, Jenkins, Naylor, Belton, and Thomas, forwards. Bradford—Gunn, back ; Bell, Surman, Scholefleld, and Leath, three-quarter backs ; Griffin and Brear, half backs ; Mann, Francis, Haley, Bees, Staiucliffe, and Laidlaw, forwards. Referee, Mr. Wood, Huddersfield. Bradford made a fair show in the opening stages of the game, but the Warriugtonians threw more energy into their work, and the forwards commanding the scrimmages better Brooks and Lloyd opened out the game. A brilliant effort between Brooks. Dickenson and Fish, notable for the rapidity of the exchanges, was negatived through the oval being passed a shade forward but a moment later the ball was whipped out to Taylor, who dashed through a ruck of opponents end scored near the posts, for Thomas to add the goal. Warrington still continued to attack desperately, and Jenkins was especially to the fore in compelling Bradford to adopt safety tactics. A long dribble by Taylor found a flaw in the defence, and Lloyd, seconding the effort scored, for Thomas to add the goal. Bradford made spasmodic efforts, but rarely became dangerous, and another renewal of the attack resulted in another , passing movement, and this time Fish, after receiving I from Dickenson, sprinted over, for Thomas to land his third goal. A smart rally by the visitors near the interval had a tangible ending, for from a penalty Gum scored a goal. At half-time Warrington led by 15 points against two. Turning round the Warrington forwards again dominated the situation, and after Dickenson had made a great effort on the right, a united attack a the forwards culminated in Belton forcing himself over. A minute later Watkin scored at the corner. In both cases Thomas failed at the place kicks, whilst a couple of shots from penalty kicks also travelled wide of the mark. By the aid of free kicks Bradford eased the pressure slightly, and Surman dropped a neat goal. Then came a brilliant individual effort by Taylor, this resulting in Dickenson scoring an unconverted try. Brooks next landed a penalty goal. Warrington were constantly on the attack, and Bradford were a hope- lessly outclassed team. In the last few minutes Dickenson put on the finishing touch to another rapid series of exchanges, and the final result was :—War- rington, four goals seven tries (29 points) ; Bradford, two goals (4 points). SHROVE TUESDAY ,MATCHES. RUNCORN BEATEN AT HALIFAX.

Halifax Twenty-three points Runcorn Halifax gave a fine exhibition of football at home, outplaying Runcorn at all points, and the victory was witnessed by 4,000 spectators. The game had been in progress a few minutes only when Thomas scored two splendid tries, which Little improved. Runcorn, after these reverses, played up pluckily, and Williams scored a try, but Richardson failed at the goal kick. Then Little kicked a penalty goal and Williams crossed the line for Halifax, which Little converted. At the interval Halifax led by seventeen points to three. fu the second half Harrison was absent hurt, and Halifax had all the play, Robinson and Thomas scoring tries.

===== 0002601_19080307_art0096.txt | included=true | included =====
DEWSBURY BEAT BARROW.

Dewsbury Five points Barrow Nothing At Dewsbury, in fine weather, before a small attend- ance, a very even game was witnessed, both sides show- ing fine defensive tactics. From loose play in front of the Burrow posts Gildes dropped a fine goal, and towards the finish Northorpe scored the only try of the match. Bland failed at goal. Gifford, Hannah, and Eloggett played well for Barrow. BATLEY TOO GOOD FOR LEEDS. .Batley Thirteen points Leeds At Leeds, before 2,000 spectators, in fine weather. Beevers and Airey (Batley) scored tries, of which Walter Davies only improved one. Young (Leeds) kicked a goal. In the second portion Birch and Chap- man both scored tries, Young kicking one goal. Walter Davies, of Batley, kicked a goal, whilst his club-mate, William Davies, scored a try.

MANCHESTER WOMAN'S 103RD BIRTHDAY.

Mrs. Ann Lucy, who attained her 103rd -year on Wed- nesday, has resided for fifty-three years in a back-to- back house in Old Mount-street, off Rochdale-road, Manchester.

ALL GIRLS. A Mrs. Pennett has given birth to triplets—girls— at Brighton. Mother and children are doing well.

JUNIOR LIBERALS.

Address on "Bad Trade."

"Bad trade " or "Hard times" was the subject of Mr. T.M.Phillips' Interesting addr ass to the Junior Liberals assembled at Gregory's Cali on Monday evening. Councillor J.Elawthorn presided. Mr. Phillips attributed bad trade to over-production, and said that 1907 was a record year so far as the trade returns were concerned, but so far as the individual trader was con- cerned he would say that trade had not been very good. Referring to the American crisis, Mr. Phillips said the Americans had been doing things too fast. That crisis would enable England to get things cheaper. Raw materials would some cheaper because the Americans wanted money and England could buy raw materials from them at a cheaper price than before the crisis. Germany was also in a bad way through trying to move too fast. One thing which caused a certain amount of bad trade was the attraction of the football ground. He thought football caused a great loss to the town, especially when mid-week matches were on. The men who left their work to go to football matches did not realise the damage they were doing both to themselves and to the trade. (Rear, hear.) Referring to personal thrift he did not think there was a more important thing. He had been amongst all classes of men in his time and he had known of men who were earning £5 a week and who were letting their families starve, whilst they themselves were spending their money foolishly. If a man, whilst good times a ere on,would put a portion of his money away for the bad times he would be able to live in comfort the whole•year round. (Hear, hear.) He suggested that they (the Junior Liberals) should take that important subject up and have a discussion on it. (Rear, hear and applause.)—The Chairman said it was getting quite a business now for men to put money in bets on football matches. it was through till football betting that referees were so frequently mobbed.—Mr. Davies moved a vote of thanks to Mr. Phillips for his interesting lecture. Mr. Guest seconded and the motion was carried, Messrs. Tilling, Barnes, Ford and Darbyshire supporting.

PADGATE.

INDUCTION OP THE REV. W. PoLLocK-HILL—The Rev. W. Pollock-Hill was instituted and inducted to the benefice of St. Thomas', Ashton-in-Makerfleld on Friday evening. The ceremony of institution to the spiritualities of the living was performed by the Lord Bishop of Liverpool, who spoke upon the relations which should exist between a shepherd and his flock, basing his com- parisons on the allegory of the Good Shepherd. The induction to the temporalities of the living was then proceeded with by the Rev. Canon Spooner, Archdeacon of Warrington. In addition to the Bishop, the Arch- deacon and the Rev. W. Pollock-11111, the following clergy were present, viz., Canon 0. Penrhyn, rural dean; Rev.W. Williams, rector ot Ashton, patron of the living ; and the Revs. H. V. Pigot, T. A. J. Ridpath, F. Ireland, G. Vale Owen, G. W. Dickinson, J. Allsop, A. Inglis, J. Kerrick, J. Llewellyn and J. H. G. Bates, curate-in-charge, while the following were present from Padgate : Mrs. Pollock-Hill, Adjutant and Mrs. Mott, and Messrs. E. V. C. London, J. Critchley, P. Royston, H. Houghton, R. Ryder and F. Blackwell.

SEQUEL TO LEAD THEFFS.

A sequel to the recent extensive thefts of lead from the tvorks of the United Alkali Company was beard at St. Helens Police Court on Monday, when William Forrest was charged with stealing 455. worth of lead. The Chief Constable said that on January 25th Police- constable Capper saw a couple of men going along Corporation-street with a handcart. He followed them, when they bolted, leaving the cart in the street. It con- tained a large quantity of lead which had been taken from the Ole-be Alkali Works. One of the men was caught, but Forrest got away. Subsequently a lot of lead was found that had been taken from the Kurtz's Works. Two men were charged and sentenced to fourteen days each, while a man who had received the lead was sent to gaol for two months. On Saturday Forrest surrendered to Police-constable Anders, remark- ing that as the other men had only got fourteen days he miaht as well get it over. He said he had a lot of beer at the time, or he would never have been there. They carried the lead along the canal bank to the cart, which was in the street. Forrest was sentenced to fourteen days on each charge.

THE SWEET TOOTH.

• The sweet tooth, possessed by every healthy youngster, is Nature's token that sugar is required for the building of the body, the bracing of tired muscles, the enriching of the brain. Currants are so remarkably rich in grape-sugar that they are now fully recognised as one of our most wholesome and sustaining foods.

Mr. Dodds and Education. Sir,—l am not so vain as to think that many, if any, of your readers will peruse my " opinion "on Mr. McKenna's Bill as inserted in your to-day's issue, but I should not like anyone to conclude that I consider the State should confine itself to the teaching of" physical , education." If you will refer to my manuscript I think , you will find that my words are "physical and intellectual education."—Yours JAMES DODDS.

February 20th. [The words " and intellectual" were omitted by a printer's error.

Ed." Es."]

Sunday Evening Concert at the Palace Theatre. Sir,—In your report of the above concert you state hat it was arranged by the Amalgamated Nlusicians' Union. Now I wish to point out that such is not the case. The concert was arranged solely by the Palace Theatre management, and the proceeds thereof after paying a fee to the artistes and orchestra went entirely to them. Thanking you in anticipation for the inser- tion of this in your valuable paper,—l remain yours truly,

F. DAVIES. (Branch Secretary of the Amalgamated Musicians' Union.) [Our correspondent is slightly mistaken. The report did not state that the concert was arranged by the Musicians' Union. hut that " the formation of an orchestra " was en early outcome of the recently estab- lished Union.—ED." Ex."]

The Licensing Bill. The Late Mr. Peter Rylands, M.P. and " The Trade." Sir,—l am sure the following extract from the " Alliance News," of October 23rd 1869, being a report of a speech made by the late Mr. Peter Rylands (M.P. for Warrington from 1868 to 1874) in the Free Trade Hall, Manchester, on October 20th 1869, will profoundly interest many of your readers. Copy Extract : Mr. Rylands, M.P., said he felt the greatest possible sympathy with the work which the Alliance was doing. He looked upon the Alliance as the only organization that was grappling with any I power with this great evil, and he could not for a I moment hesitate to render any assistance to the Alliance. We had a great deal to do in dealing with the liquor traffic. It was a traffic we had a right to deal with. Every part of the public was suffering from its operations. The taxes of the country were enormously increased in consequence of this trade. The rates were doubled, trebled, and quadrupled by this trade. These traffickers existed in every street, and they would fight for their privileges and their interests to the very utmost. On the other side was an earnest band of men anxious to promote the welfare of the community. The struggle was no mean matter. It meant simply this—will the country overcome the drink traffic ? or shall the drink traffic overcome the country ? Whether in Parliament or not, in every position cf life, his voice and his vote would be on the side of those who were struggling to overth.ow the liquor traffic."—Yours CONSISTENT.

Temperance Reform. Sir,—Over the nom-de-plume of "Phil Squod " in your issue of last Saturday a correspondent made use of the following statement :—" So long as the publican is allowed to pursue his nefarious trade unchecked,so long will men go wrong and poverty abound." Perhaps I am mistaken, but I take it that the writer wishes to infer that poverty is mainly caused by drunkenness. If my conclusion is wrong, I apologise. If I have guessed correctly I am afraid " Phil Squod " is guilty of shoddy thinking. We must all admit that much suffer- ing and crime comes from excessive drinking. But is there not another side to the question which is too often left unexamined ? We would venture to ask how much drinking comes from suffering? Mr. Holmes,the well-known city missionary, says : When I first came to London I thought drink was the root of all evil. I have always been a teetotaller, and a pretty bigoted one, and it took me just one year to unlearn some of the convictions which I had not gained from experi- ence, but adopted. Now,when I find drink given as the cause of a crime,l ask 'idly does the offender drink? And in thousands of cases I have found that drink is not a cartse but an effect." There's the rub. The need to work early and late, from precocious childhood to premature dotage, on starvation wages, in unhealthy employments, and dismal, insanitary surroundings— what is the moral effect of all that ? Not to develop noble aims and high ideals ; but to crush the soul and cramp the brain, and reduce the victims to the state of beasts of burden. 'the remedy for drunkenness is national interest in life. Give a man something, anything, to live for—congenial work, a healthy ambition, a happy home, a sympathetic woman's love—and he will need no ribbon of blue to pull him out of the degradation of drunkenness.—Yours in earnest,

W. MIIRBY. 19, Gaskell-street, Stockton Heath, March 3rd, 190 S.

The Temperance Striking Parrallel. Sir,—One frequently wonders if the multifarious associations, etc., really have any direct influence on the public opinion. There are so many voices crying in the wilderness that the result seems oftener to be a Babel of sound. In connection, however, with the Temperance Bill just introduced, a very striking exception may be noticed. In December of 1907 the Temperance Legislation League formulated a scheme of reform which when placed in parallel columns with what is suggested in the Bill shows shrewdly the Bill was anticipated. March: 3, 1908.

The Brierlby Case. Sir,—With your kind permission I should like to make plain my position with regard to the motion standing in my name at the Council meeting on Tuesday last. Some of the members seemed to think and one even expressed the opinion that it was a personal attack on two officials. I have noticed several times during the last two years that if one is apt to give even a little friendly criticism he is at once accused of being persinal, but I suppose such is the penalty of public life. The interruption I was subject to during my moving of the motion was enough to upset even old stagers, let alone a mere bantling like myself, and several members afterwards expressed their detestation of it. My reference to the new system of book-keeping embodying the idea of the Borough Treasurer was also inferred by some to be a personal attack on that official, a gentle- man for whom I have the highest regard, both as regards ability and personal honour. I said that the books could be made to shew things right, whether the stock was right or not and to this statement I adhere. What is wanted, and I am speaking from experience which is worth more I think than any elementary ideas of some of the members, is a thorough up-to-date system of stock-keeping such as is in adoption in large public works and railway companies, backed up by periodical checking. If such is not done stock-taking comes a perfect farce and not worth the paper written on. This is all I contend; surely there is nothing perst cat about this. I neither wish nor desire to cast a,

ADJOURNED LICENSING SESSIONS.

BRIDGE INN TRANSFER REFUSED. -- Condemnation of the Credit System.

The adjourned licensing sessions were held at the Warrington Police Court yesterday morning and con- siderable interest was taken in the business. The Mayor presided and the other magistrates present were : Aldermen Tinnion and Bennett, Councillor Evans, Dr. Fox, Messrs. John Fairclough, W. Furness, S. Wright, C. Jackson Holmes, H. Thornton, J. Maginn, G. Pilling, H. Roberts, W. Roberts, J. W. Hayward, John White, T. Locker. W. Richardson. The Licensing Committee consists of : The Mayor, Alderman Tinnion, Councillor Wilkinson, Dr. Fox, Messrs. John Fakelough, J. Maginn, J. W. Hayward, H. Roberts, S. Wright, W. Richardson and C. J. Holmes.

APPLICATIONS GRANTED. Mr. E. Draper applied on behalf of Lewis Richard Rowson for removal of the wine and spirit (off) licence from the Market Place to Barbauld-street. The appli- cation was granted at the annual sessions, subject to confirmation at the adjourned meeting. The application was granted. Mr. Browne applied for the renewal of an off-licence in Folly-lane in the name of Samuel Jenks. Be pointed out that the matter of temporary authority was adjourned at the annual meeting as Mr. Steel said the executors objected. They had since come to terms and temporary authority had been granted to Mr. Jenks who was the son of the owner.

The application N-s -as granted. OFF-LICENCE OBJECTED TO. BEER ON CREDIT.

The Clerk announced that the next business was the hearing of the objection to the off-licence of Robert John Ellis, 110, Liverpool-road. Mr. Cuthbert Smith, barrister (instructed by Mr. T. S. Steel) appeared for the licencee, Mr. Rigby Swift, barrister (instructed by Messrs. Arthur Browne and Co.) for the owners (Messrs. Greenall, Whitley arid Co.), and Mr. T. S. Steel for the Off-Licence Holders' Association. Mr. Swift asked the Bench to conclude the applica- tion with regard to the Bridge inn transfer before hearing the objection. He did not think the application would take more than a minute or two. Mr. Batty, solicitor, Manchester: lam instructed to oppose the application. lam prepared with witnesses and I aril sure the Bench won't shut out the opposition of residents in the district. Mr. Swift: I understand this is the same application I made on the 7th of last month. The application was not opposed but the Bench thought I ought to make some further offer and it was adjourned on that account. I am prepared now to tell the Bench in half-a-dozen words what I am prepared to do. The Magistrates' Clerk : That does not preclude opposition. The application was adjourned. The Bench decided to hear the objection to the off- licence first. The Chief Constable said that as directed by the Bench at the annual licensing meeting he served a notice on Ellis stating that the renewal of the licence was objected to on the grounds that the licence was not required to meet the wants of the neighbourhood, and that irregularities had occurred in the conduct of the premises. Complaints were received by the police in August and September last with regard to the way in which the premises were being conducted. He inter- viewed the tenant on the 20th September, and Ellis made the statement which had been set out in his (the Chief Constable's) annual report to the licensing justices, to the effect that there was a custom in existence with regard to the granting of credit. He told Ellis that in his (the Chief Constable's) opinion it was very undesirable tha t such a practice should exist, as it was demoralizing and most degrading. Ellis replied that it was a practice that had been carried on for a considerable time past and he went on to say that quite a number of licence holders in the borough did the same thing and that it was with the knowledge of the brewers. He (the Chief Constable) asked Ellis to go home and make a note of what he had told him and he promised to do so. Nothing further happened until be (the Chief Constable) reported it to the Licensing Committee. It would appear that the practice had been carried on ever since the time that he had the interview with Mr. Ellis. Mr. Cuthbert Smith : Have you got the complaint there ?—Yes. I would like to see it.—The complaint that was made was not altogether of the same thine that it turned out to be on inquiries being made. That is why I want to see it—lt is a confidential communication and unless the Bench direct it, I shall refuse to allow you to see it. Do I understand that in fact the complaint was about something which you have not found to be correct ?—No, that is not so. lam making the corn- I plaint now. I am not taking it upon behalf of the person who makes a complaint. What you are complaining of is the system of credit which this man, you say, gives to his customers and 1 which he has admitted to you ?—Yes. The complaint went further than that, didn't it ?—i In what respect ? You have told the Bench that there was something in the complaint which you did not find on inquiryl from the man but that he admitted that he gave credit? —The complaint was that a system of credit was in existence but it was given in a different form from what the actual facts are. Is that complaint in writing I—lt is. Then I object absolutely to you giving evidence as to what is in that complaint unless it is produced.—l am giving evidence on the interview I had with Mr. Ellis, which was led rip to by the fact of this com- plaint being received. Mr. Rigby Swift : May I identify myself with the point raised by Mr. Cuthbert Smith ? It is trivial in the extreme for the witness to come and say "I received a complaint "and not to produce the complaint, while admitting that he has got it in his possession. As a matter of law we are entitled to have it. As a matter of commonsense what object can there be in the Chief Constable keeping it back ? The Mayor : The decision of the Bench is that you must confine yourself to the interview with Mr. Ellis— to what you know of your own knowledge. Further cross-examined by Mr. Cuthbert Smith, the Chief Constable said Ellis admitted keeping an account book. He (Mr. Nicholls) did not suggest that Ellis issued books to the men in the works. The man whom he trusted and who was responsible for the debts had the book and handed it to the workmen. Have you any evidence of that ?—Mr. Ellis's own

statement. The Chief Constable further stated that Ellis admitted having been cautioned previously with regard to the complaint by the police, and he (Mr. Nicholls) wcis in a position to put evidence in the box that he had been cautioned on no less than two occasions on the same matter. Mr. Mnginn : Ellis admitted to you that he was aware it was wrong to sell drink on credit ?—No. He I rather said there was nothing at all wrong in it and he

intended to continue it. Asked as to Ellis's statement with reference to the brewers, the Chief Constable said he rather questioned it at the time and told him he did not think it was possible the brewers could know of it. Ellis said it Was so. The Mayor asked whether the credid system had been stopped since the Chief Constable made the complaint to the Bench? The Chief Constable : I am not in a position to say that and I don't think [ought to mention an anonymous communication I have had. Mr. Crompton,of the Borough Surveyor's department, produced a plan of the district and said there was one licence to 422 of the population. The average for the town was one for every 353. Mr. Cuthbert Smith said Mr. Ellis had held the licence for nine years and had been a licencee in War- rington for ten or eleven years. With regard to the knowledge of the brewers, he thought Mr. Ellis had been a little misunderstood in what he said, but that did not affect the question much. So far as the needs of the neighbourhood were concerned,Ellis camo within the test applied by the magistrates at the annual sessions, because he sold about ten barrels per week and 4 dozen of bottled beer. The credit system was one which the licencee found in existence when he came there and he did not realize that he was doing tanything—nor did he (Mr.Smith)realize that he was doing anything—contrary to any Act of Parliament or to any provision of the Licensing Act. Heconsidered he was just as much entitled to sell beer on credit as a wine merchant was to sell wine. While he was not anxious to give it up—because it had been an old standing custom and he thought it would he a great hardship if he had to give it up while other licencees were doing the same thing—if it was wish of the Bench that he should discontinue it, he would do so. Ellis denied that he was any party to the arrangement whereby the man who sold the beer in the works made a profit of id per pint. Mr. Rigby Swift said Messrs. Gresnall had always recognized that and had discour• aged their tenants from giving credit.
