The Examiner, Saturday, March 7, 1903.

WARRINGTON MUSICAL SOCIETY.

" Faust."

In times past the Musical Society has had a well- founded grievance against the Warrington public because of the not very generous support given to the annual series of classical concerts, but this season the Society has every reason to be gratified at the success which has attended each of the three concerts. The first consisted of the production for the first time in Warrington of Sir Edward Elgar's great work " The Dream of Gerontius " before a crowded house; the second was the popular "Messiah " concert at Christmas ; and on Tuesday night Gounod a "Faust " was given for the concluding concert of the season. Again there was a magnificent audience, and an evi- dently warm appreciation of the Society's efforts "Faust " is, of COUTSO, not new either to the Society or to Warrington, but Gounod's exquisite setting of the romantic story has not been given for several years and it was particularly welcome on Tuesday evening. The interest in the event was certainly added to by the personnel of the principals announced to take part. Three were well-known members of the ' Society—Mrs. Arthur Booth, Miss Mabel Henshall and Mr.W. H. Wallington—whose position as principals in so important a musical venture was an honour alike to themselves and the Society with which they have been so long and so closely identified. The other two soloists were Mr. Charles Tree (a favourite bass) and Mr. Walter Hyde, a young tenor who has leaped at a bound to the front rank of the musical profession. When the Society engaged him a few months ago he was a tenor who was noted as gradu- ally coming to the front, and, very luckily for War- rington people, he has "arrived." Mr. Hyde has within the last few weeks won golden opinions from the London critics for his singing in the " Ring " performances at Covent Garden, and there was con- sequently a keen interest in his debut at Warrington. We need hardly say that Mr. Hyde fulfilled the highest expectations in his role Qf Faust. He possesses a beautifully clear and flexible tenor voice, capable of very wide ranee, and his singing on Thursday was characterised by flawless intonation, admirable articu- lation' and deep expression. In the frequent numbers which fell to his lot as Faust Mr. Hyde's capabilities were fully employed, and he earned the warmest applause. His great opportunity was in the cavatina " All hail thou dwelling pure and lowly," which was sung with the passion and feeling of a true artiste, and called forth a storm of applause. Mr. Chas. Tree makes an ideal Mephis- topheles, and one cannot escape the desire to see him attired for the part as it would be depicted on the operatic stage, for Mr. Tree can look really Mephistophelian even in the severe garb of the concert platform. As a vocalist he is of the highest class, and his rendering of "Clear the way for the calf of gold" and the sardonic serenade " Catarina, while you play at sleeping "were items which fully brought out his capacity for dramatic yet tuneful vocalisation. Mrs. Booth was called upon for a really exacting and sustained effort to sing the part of Margarita, and we must say candidly that she acquitted herself brilli- antly, to the no small satisfaction of an audience with which she was unmistakably popular. At first she betrayed a slight tremulousness which affected the rendition of " O'er the sea in Thule of old," but in the trying jewel-song Mrs. Booth rose superior to what may have been pardonable nervousness, and sang this famous number with a joyous abandon and technical skill alike admirable. She later distinguished herself in the duet " The hour is late," the appeal " Yet once again beloved," and the melody " No, my lord, not a lady am I." We have nothing but praise also for the debut of Miss Henshall in serious opera. Though she possesses a contralto voice essentially light, yet it is wonderfully sweet and tune- ful and was used to capital affect in Siebel's dainty songs " Gentle flowers in the dew," and " When all was young, and pleasant May was blooming." Miss Henshall is blessed with an enviable confidence, and she sang with an easy grace and clear articulation which won for her the warmest encomiums. Of Mr. W. FLWallington it is hardly possible to speak so highly. He was handicapped by the effects of a throat- cold and in the declamatory challenge to Faust, where so much is demanded of the, vocal organs he was unable to do himself or the musical theme full justice. But in " Dear gift of my sister," and "Even bravest hearts may swell" Mr. Wallington sang very tunefully. We need say little about the work of the chorus which, in " Faust " is not very hAavy, and to the Warrington Society not unfamiliar. The chorus was as usual powerful and well-balanced, thou,gh one could detect a slight straining on the part of the tenors in some of the high notes which suggested the need for reinforcements. The famous ' Soldiers' Chorus was particularly well rendered, being full of inspiration, and markedly precise. The orchestra acquitted itself with distinction, and if we have one fault to find it was that both the tenor and contralto soloists suffered at times from the undue assertiveness and volume of the instrumental music at their backs Mr. F. H. Crossley conducted with his well-known skill and exercised a marked control which was clearly effictfcious in maintaining the balance of both choral and orchestral work.

SIIFFRAGISI BADGE OF HONOUR. A badge of honour in the form of a metal brooch hearing upon it a reproduction of the main entrance to Holloway Gaol was on Thursday evening presented to each of the ten members of the Women's Freedom League recently released from prison following con- victions for the demonstrations outside the houses of members of the Cabinet.

Now, if these figures were British we should find the Tariff Reform League telling us that we were well on the way to ruin because we had no " Tariff Reform "to shut out foreign goods. It is most clear that Germany imports nearly £100,000,000 worth of manufactured goods a y ear, and that, therefore, these goods go in in spite of Protection. It seems a cruel thing to deprive British Tariff Reformers of their darling belief that it is possible to shut out imports of manufactures by a trumpery duty of 10 per cent., as suggested by Mr. Chamberlain at Glasgow. Worse remain, to tell. _ _

rhe ease of the United States. Let us see whether the case of America is any different. America, of course, has a much heavier tariff than that of Germany. The American duties are so much bigger that Germany may almost be called a Free Trade country as compared with America. Does the Dinghy Tariff shut out manu- factured goods ? I take the following facts from the official Statistical Abstract of the United States, published at Washington :

IMPORTS OF MANUFACTURED GOODS INTO

Here, again, are figures calculated to astonish the poor Tariff Reformer. In 1906 the United States imported manufactured goods worth 528,000,000 dollars, or about £103,000,000. This great quantity of manufactures managed to scramble over the tariff wall. And this in spite of duties, some of which are exceedingly tall. Surely it is for tariff reformers to ask themselves : What is the use as a barrier of Mr. Chamberlain's 10 per cent. if the famous Dingley Tariff cannot do better than this ? What does it avail us to believe that a milk and water tariff would shut out go ,cl 3 if one of the most severe tariffs in the world allows in 2,105,000,000 worth of g ocls in a single year ?

The American Figure Really Z 120,030,000

As a matter of fact, the American figures given understate the case. The American system of valuing imports differs from that practised in Britain and Germany. The German figures given above, like our own, include cost of freight and insurance to port of entry—i.e., they are what are called c.i.f. values. Tlata American figures, howeyer, are taken exclusive of freight and insurance, so that we need to add to them on that account. Valued, as the German figures are valued, American imports of manufactures are worth about ,120,000,000 per annum.

The Explanation.

This being the opening day, interest centred round the addresses of the retiring President, Dr. Bendel Harris, and the incoming President, Dr. David Brooks, of Southport. The Council always expects something fresh from this Bible scholar and archreologist, Dr. Harris, and they were not disappointed. It was certainly refreshing to leave behind the things of which everyone is thinking,such as Socialism, poverty, the drink traffic, "contracting out," &c., and be led to the great Hittite centre of Boghaz-Keni in search of ancient documents to throw light upon the Bible story, and to study the aromatic papyri found at Assuan. It was a startling suggestion that the Free Churches should aid in the work of exploration, and yet why not ? Anything that can shed additional light on Bible records is of the first importance to Christians of all Churches. ! Dr. Brooks in his address brought us back to more ! familiar topics. He appropriately commenced by a ! touching reference to him who was expected to occupy the chair but whose familiar voice with its delightful Scotch burr was, as far as this life is concerned, for ! ever silent. The sympathy of all went out to Dr. Brooks when he expressed how inadequately he felt himself to be in trying to fill the large gap made by 1 the death of lan Alliclaren. The tribute to Dr. Watson was worthy of the occasion. Part of it was as follows : The Free Churches, and not they alone, recognised in

DR. JOHN WATSON a great minister of the Gospel. The loftiness of his patriotic and civic ideals, and his clear-sighted courage in their pursuit, made him a great force for righteousness. More than all this, he had ever a tender sympathy for all who were fighting a hard battle, or had lokt in the struggle and were sore with defeat. 'None knew better than he how narrow is the watershed where the question is determined whether a career shall lead out into the sunshine of success, or shall be thwarted and pass into the shadows. This sympathy of his gave encouragement and hope to all with whom he came into personal contact. In his writings he had a yet wider scope, and he extended his friendships and his mission all over the English-speaking world. What a message we mizht have had from such a man! He, being dead, yet speaketh. The whole address was an insistence on duties rather than on rights, although the rights were not omitted as the following passage will show : Dissenters, as most of us used to be called, from the very necessities of the case, have been accustomed to insistence on their rights. And this insistence must continue. It is true that, in spite of disadvan- tages and disabilities, the Free Churches have become strong and influential, until they represent half the religious community. That fact makes the State's attitude in favouring one denomination obviously absurd as well as intolerably oppressive. THS HOUSE OF LORDS

is a good—or a bad—illustration. It is the most mischievous anachronism of our country and our time. There you have a bench of Bishops of one Church, and not a single individual, either clerical or lay, who is a member of any Free Church of England or Wales. Such a state of things at the beginning of the twentieth century is grotesque. It is clear that there can be no pause in the assertion of Free Church rights until the absurdities, of which this is only a specimen, are swept away. There will be none until so far as the State is concerned, absolute equality of all religious denominations is completely guaranteed. The following passage emphasizing the mother instinct which ought to characterize all Church work I thought very fine : - And this suggestion of obvious duty harmonises with the very strongest instinct of a true Church of Christ. That instinct is MOTHERHOOD. The Church is the incarnation of that spirit which looked upon crowded Jerusalem and sobbed out maternal pity. " How often would I have gathered thy children together, oven as a hen gathereth her chickens under her wings, and ye would not." It is the Good-Shepherd spirit which counts each individual of value, notes each wanderer from the fold, is pained by the absence, and reckons no sacrifice too great to rescue and restore. This Shepherd-Spirit neither slumbers nor sleeps. It tenderly watches the interests of the people from the cradle to the grave' It is Catholic in its manifestation. No class is above its attention, and none is beneath it. Tts sympathy is boundless, its patience infinite. This is the Christ- Spirit incarnate in His Church. Dr. Brooks, while speaking only for himself, left no doubt about his feeling with reference to what is called

The explanation of what would appear to Tariff Reformers extraordinary facts is exceedingly instruc- tive. The imports of manufactured goods into Germany and AmPrica are necessary to Germany and America, just as our imports are neceseary to us. The Tariff Reformer when he talks wildly of inapoits causing unemployment forgets that they are imported by people who need them. It is only when it is realised that the greater part of the " manufactures " imported into any country are raw materials of indus- try, that the impossibility of shutting out manufac- tures is also realised. Factories must buy from abroad if they are to pursue their activities, for no country produces the bast and cheapest of known manufactures in every branch of every trade. When we put on duties, therefore, hoping to do good, we often do more harm than good by raising the price of materials of industry. That ie why we find that of 375 boot and shoe manufacturers of New England, U.S.A., 311 pronounced in layout- of giving up the boot duty if the hide duty were also abolished, while of 40 New England tanners 20 wee in favour of giving up the leather duty if only the hide duty were also got rid of. You see a hide duty hits the tanner and a hide duty or a leather duty hits the bootmaker, while a boot duty hits men in all trades for all men ria&l b) ots. Protection is a vicious circle which vainly seeks to make everyone 'better off by making everything dearer.

===== 0002601_19080307_art0120.txt | included=true | included =====
The Tippeal to Sections.

To th• cement maker the Protectionist promises dearer cement. But dearer cement hits the building trade. To the carpenter Protection promises a duty on imported joinery. But the carpenter is not reminded that dearer cement, dearer glass, dearer iron, dearer glue, dearer nails, dearer everything, mean dearer houses, fewer houses, and, therefore, less and not more work for carpenters. To.the spinner Protection promises a better price for yarn. But a better price for yarn hits the weaver, who in his turn. has to be placated with a duty to enable him to get a better price for his cloth. This particular point caused a big quarrel in the arrangement of the new .German tariff, when spinners and weavers fouzlit each other like the famous Kil- kenny cats, and in the end neither German spinner nor German weaver was satisfied. My advice to the man who is kindly offered a tax to help him raise the prices of his own particular product is that he should inquire what sort of taxes the other trades which affect him have been promised, and then to weigh very carefully whether be stands to gain anything through the privilege of buying dearly everything that he needs.

Pree Trade the True Protection.

In Yiew of all these considerations, Free Trade— the right, that is, to buy what we need in the best market—remains the only commen sense policy. It is the true " Protection " of the manufacturer, because

===== 0002601_19080307_art0121.txt | included=true | included =====
"TME SECULAR SOLUTION."

It will be a great national disgrace if through "lazi- ness " we are driven to a solution which the great majority of the nation would on its merits repudiate. Dr. Brooks said :

For myself, I believe that this work can only be properly discharged in a spirit of reverence and by association with distinctively Christian teaching. It will be scandal to the common Christianity of all the Churches if our disagreements should seem to make this impossible. I know that a portion Eif our Council feels that agreement is hopeless, and that Christian teaching must be left to Christian Churches. A still larger number of men outside all the Churches have lazily accepted this apparently easy way out of the difficulty. To these ranks the Bishop of Manchester adds a fresh battalion with every speech which he makes on the question. To me, however, the secular solution, as it is called, has always seemed an obvious futility. That can be no solution which will leave in bitter dissatisfaction fully one-half the religious people of England.

THE POVERTY QUESTION

is no new one but it is being faced with a new serious- ness and from a new point of view. We are beginning to realize that the state of poverty in which so many merely exist is not normal, is unnatural. In times gone by we were accustomed to look upon disease, plague, &c., as visitations of God which were outside the control of man. This attitude has entirely passed away. In the same way the condition of those whose whole energy has to be given to providing the necessaries to keep body and soul together is being faced from a new standpoint and we are now looking upon such a condition as abnormal and as a challenge to our twentieth century civilization. The President's words if they did not suggest a remedy certainly recognized that a remedy must be found: Here are the broad fields white for harvesting. Whenever the Church tries to see the multitude with the eyes of Christ, pity, sorrow, indignation, and impatience battle in her heart. With all that has been done the honest labourer still gets less than any reasonable share of the amenities of modern life. While a few thousands in England are surfeited with luxuries millions are living on the verge of want, or are already precipitated into the abyss. The conscience and heart of the Church demands action wise, bold, and swift. There was also a clear recognition of what is apt to be forgotten or get out of proportion when so many eyes are looking to collective and legislative action. Dr. Brooks said : Yet when all has been said, the higher concern of a Church of Christ is the CHARACTER OF THE PEOPLE. _

It is important to secure for all men civic, social, and economic rights. It is still more important that they should deserve them. We earnestly desire that men should have better houses to live in ; still more we desire that those men who live in them should be better men. We can be no parties to economic theories and methods which will weaken the founda- tions of chavicter. For Chronio Chest Complaint, Woods' Gres Peppermint Cure.—Advt.

LOCAL PARLIAMENT. OLD-AGE PENSIONS PASSED. A Home Rule Bill The usual meeting of our Local Parliament was held in the Co-operative Hall on Thursday evening. There was a large attendance of members. The Clerk of the House (Mr. Mason) was absent but his place was filled by Mr. Nathan Cook, jun., deputy clerk. After the questions had been dealt with the debate on the Old-Age Pensions Bill was resumed. The MEMBER FOR OSWESTRY (Mr. W. L. Williams) asked the Government in what way they intended to broaden the basis of taxation in order to raise the money required for old-age pensions ?" He expresssd the concern of the party he represented for the welfare of the workers, and criticised the" miserable pittance" of 4s. or ss. per week which was available as the super- annuation fund from trade unions after 20 or 30 years' membership. The speaker, however, offered no criticism of the Bill before the House. The COLONIAL SECRETARY, Mr. W. Furness, said fariff Reform was mentioned by the Tories with the idea of bringing the Colonies closer to the Mother , Country. They (the Liberal party) believed that the Colonies were quite able and strong enough to look after theruselves.alr.Furness went on to further criticise Tariff Reform as a measure for keeping people fully employed at good wages, until called to order by the Speaker. He concluded by saying that it would be most pernicious to introduce a scheme for Old-Age Pensions which would place a tax upon the working classes of this country, as had been suggested by a Member opposite. The MEMBER FOR CARLOW (Mr. A. M. Crowe) con- gratulated the Government upon having the courage to tackle this acute question in a practical manner. The Tories wished to turn the question into one of Tariff Reform, but if the Old-Age Pensions had to wait until the Fiscal Policy of the country was altered it would be a lasting disgrace. (Applause.) Let the Government tackle Land Values and alter the levyiug of the Ineome Tax. (Hear, hear.) Money would not then be wanting to carry the question into effect. Let the Government give self-government to Ireland and they would save immediately four millions for the national exchequer. (Applause.) Old-Age Pensions would also save the two millions expended in out-door relief. He supported the Bill. The MEMBER FOR PETERSFIELD (Mr. F. G. Taylor) moved an amendment to clause 1 to insert the words "out of a tax to be called the Pension Tax to be levied upon all wages or other income." After expressing his willingness to support Old-Age Pensions he said they were told that it would require £26,000,000 for the Bill, and he thought the Government might have introduced some practical scheat e for raising the money instead of offering an impractical remedy. None of the members of ' the Government seemed to be agreed upon the method by which the money was to be rnised.lt appeared to him that a scheme which did not call for con- tributions from all the people in the country could not be considered sound. He blamed the unfortunate absence of thrift among the people who desired and I needed an old-age pension.("No.")Where everybody was to benefit everybody should contribute. A half-penny in the £ on the wages of all would produce a consider- able sum. There was plenty of money available. What about the money spent by tontine clubs in picnics and suppers (hear, hear and laughter)—and by the general I public on sport, music halls, tobacco, etc., and by trade unionists in levies ? There was a possibility of making provision for pensions through the friendly societies. Those people who desired to be safeguarded in the I future should join such societies in early life. The MEMBER FOR HORNSBY (Mr. A. Keenan) seconded the amendnfent The PRESIDENT OF THE LOCAL GOVERNMENT BOARD (Mr. Morton) said he hoped no Liberal Government would ever introduce the system embodied in the amendment—the system of direct taxation of the work- ingmen—so long as the present industrial conditions continued to exist. (Applause). They needed only to endorse the principle of old-age pensions. The money would be a matter for the Chancellor of the Exchequer. The MEMBER FOR NORMANTON (Mr. Nightingale) also criticised strongly the suggestion to draw contributions from the earnings of the poor. Speaking for the Socialists he said, We are the coming force' and made an impassioned appeal to the political parties to sink politics and help those below them. The amendment was then put and defeated. The MEMBER FOR BLACKBURN (Mr. R. Pierce Williams) proposed an amendment to Clause 2. to the effect that the money should be drawn from a graduated and differentiated tax on incomes of over £2,000 and out of the revenue from death duties Not a fraction of the cost of pensions, he said, should be borne by the workers themselves. The proposal to tax the moneyed classes did not arise from the predatory instinct attributed to the party to which he belonged. (Laughter and applause.) The wages of the working classes did not provide them with a margin that could be taxed. Five million persons took half the income of the people, and the other half was divided among the remaining thirty-eight millions. Fourteen million people only earned an average of £45 a year, and "the money must corns from where it is—from the rich." (Applause.) To offer the working classes Tariff Reform was merely to lift the burden from one shoulder to another. (Cries of "no " and applause.) The MEMBER POE WEST LEEDS (Mr. D. Shires) seconded, and pointed out that taxing the wealth of the country meant taxing everybody, because the workers produced the wealth.

- The MEMP.ER FOR LAMBETH (Mr. J. C. Wilkinson) '." said society was divided into two classes—the working and the shirking classes. (Laughter.) They must tax the idlers and the rich. It was impossible to tax the , unemployed, and people on the verge of starvation. . The amendment of the Member for Blackburn was then put, and carried by a large majority. The MEMBER FOR CAMBRIDGE (Mr. H. Hope) intro- duced another amendment to levy a small weekly pay- ment on workingmen and women from 16 years of age, and an equal amount from their employers; the Govern- ment to add a sum of say £2 12s. yearly to each pen- sion. Clause 2as it stood avant, he said. universal ti pauperism. The money required by the 13111 could ' never he raised except by universal contribution. The MEMBER FOR WEST CLARE (Mr. Felix Maginn) seconded this amendment because he was convinced it was.the only scheme which was applicable now.A general' contribution would ensure the success of the scheme by giving every workingman an interest in it, and would take away the taint of pauperism. The amendment was lost. The PREMIER (Mr. Upson) in winding up the debate vouched for the sincerity of the Government in intro- ducing the Bill. There had been no -serious criticism of it until the last amendment. The Bill might have been attacked on the ground that it was non-contribu- trny and non-discriminating. Instead, attention had been devoted mainly to the monetary side of the Bill, which was really a matter for the Chancellor of the Exchequer. If the Opposition were in favour of old- , age pensions as they professed they must vote for this 1, Bill. How the money was to be raised was not ' for them to decide. So far as they knew it might be raised by protective tariffs. (Loud laughter and cheers.) The Bill was to endow necessitous old , I age, and they merely regarded it as a palliative until the fruits of labour should be more evenly distributed and every man was able to provide for old age. (Cheers.) After adding that experts were agreed that a discriminating scheme was absolutely impossible in such a country as this, the Premier moved that" the question be now put." The SPEAKER asked for a vote on the Bill as read and it was carried without a single dissentient. The PREMIER announced amid cheers from the Government Benches that when they had got this and the next Bill out of the way, he was prepared to afford facilities for a full dress debate on the question of Free Trade v. Protection. THE HOME RULE BILL. The SECRETARY FOR IRELAND (Mr. J. F. Awbery) then read, amid cheers from the Nationalists, the text of the Home Rule Bill which he proposed to introduce at the next meeting. The Bill, which contains eleven clauses, is, of con- _ . . .._ . .. .

, i siderable length. Briefly put it enacts that" Ireland , shall be governed, controlled and managed so far as it relates to its local and domestic affairs by a statutory - 1 Parliament holding its sittings in Ireland ; That such ' Parliament shall in no wise interfere with matters ' concerning Ireland which may be of an imperial , character, or effecting the solidity and preservation of the Empire." Further clauses enact that l the King shall exercise all privilege vested / in the Crown, that a second chamber shall be con- stituted for ten years, its permanency or dissolution afterwards to be decided by a Royal Commission ; that for a certain period there shall be an Exchequer grant to Ireland : that the Irish Parliament shall send 50 representatives to the English Parliament ; local Government. taxation, &c.. to be vested in the Parlia- ment, and "Dublin Castle Government" to be abolished: in respect of differences between the two chambers, an independent Commission nominated by the Sovereign shall settle matters. Next week's meeting of the Society will be held on , Tuesday evening. ..,
